PREFACE

This Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India has been
prepared for submission to the Governor under Article 151 of the Constitution
for being laid before the State Legislature.

The Report, covering the year 2011-12, contains significant results of the
compliance audits and performance audits of the Departments of the
Government of Madhya Pradesh under Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector
including Departments of Forest, Narmada Valley Development, Public
Works and Water Resources. However, Departments under the General, Social
and Revenue Sectors are excluded and covered in the Reports on the General,
Social and Revenue Sectors.

The cases mentioned in the Report are among those which came to notice in
the course of test audit of accounts during the year 2011-12 as well as those
which came to notice in earlier years but could not be reported in previous
Audit Reports; matters relating to the period subsequent to 2011-12 have also
been included, wherever necessary.

Audit has been conducted in conformity with the Auditing Standards issued by
the Comptroller and Auditor General of India, based on the auditing standards
of the International Organisation of Supreme Audit Institutions.

Chapter 1 of this report narrates the planning and extent of audit, responses of
the departments to draft paragraphs and Audit Reports and provides a synopsis
of significant audit observations included in this Report. Chapter 2 deals with
the findings of Performance Audit on Development of Roads in Madhya
Pradesh and two thematic studies on Construction of Minor [rrigation
Schemes in Wainganga Basin and Distribution Network of Rani Avanti Bai
Lodhi Sagar Project respectively, while Chapter 3 contains findings on
Department Centric Audit of Narmada Valley Development Authority.
Chapter 4 deals with audit findings of compliance audit in various
Departments.

vi



Chapter 1

Introduction



Chapter I: Intraduction

CHAPTER-I

Introduction

11 About this Report

This Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India (C&AG) relates
to matters arising from performance audit of selected programmes and
activities and compliance audit of Government departments and autonomous
bodies falling under economic sector in the state of Madhya Pradesh.

Performance Audit (PA) is undertaken to ensure whether the Government
programmes have achieved the desired objectives at the minimum cost and
given intended benefit. In the last few years, Audit has reported on several
significant deficiencies in implementation of various programme/activity
through Performance Audit as well as audit of selected departments.
Compliance audit refers to examination of the transactions relating to
expenditure of the audited entities to ascertain whether the provisions of the
Constitution of India, applicable laws, rules, regulations and various orders
and instructions issued by competent authorities are being complied with and
the objectives of the programme/activity/depariment are achieved
economically and efficiently.

The primary purpose of the Report is to bring to the notice of the State
Legislature, important results of audit. The findings of audit are expected to
enable Executive to take corrective actions as also to frame policies and
directives that will lead to improved financial management of the
organisations, thereby, contributing to better governance.

This chapter in addition to explaining the profile of audited entities, the
planning and extent of audit, provides a synopsis of the significant deficiencies
and achievements in implementation of selected schemes, significant audit
observations made during the audit of transactions and follow-up on previous
Audit Reports. Chapter-2 of this report contains findings arising out of
performance audit and audit on selected themes and Chapter-3 of this report
contains Department Centric audit of Narmada Valley Development
Department. Chapter-4 deals with significant finding relating from the
compliance audit of various departments under Economic Sector.

1.2 Audited Entity Profiles

There are 56 departments in the State at the Secretariat level, headed by
Additional Chief Secretaries/Principal Secretaries/Secri es, who are
assisted by Commissioner/Directors and subordinate officers under them. Of
these, 15 departments including Public Sector Units (PSUs)/Autonomous
bodies/ Local Bodies coming under these departments, are under the audit
jurisdiction of the Accountant General (Economic & Revenue Sector Audit)
Bhopal. These departments were covered for audit for inclusion of audit
findings in this Audit Report.

1.3. Authority for Audit

The authority for audit by the C&AG is derived from Articles 149 and 151 of

the Constitution of India and the Comptroller and Auditor General’s (Duties,

Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971. C&AG conducts audit of
expenditure of the Departments and Autonomous Bodies of Government of

Madhya Pradesh under Section 131, 147, 15°, 191 and 20° of the C&AGs
(DPC) Act. The Principles and methodologies for various audits are prescribed
in the Auditing Standards and the Regulations on Audit and Accounts, 2007
issued by the C&AG.

14 Organisational Structure of the Accountant General (Economic

and Revenue Sector Audit), Madhya Pradesh

As a part of restructuring of State Audit Offices by the CAG, erstwhile office
of the Accountant General (Works and Receipt Audit), Madhya Pradesh
became the office of Accountant General of the Economic Services and
Revenue Services Departments of the Government of Madhya Pradesh and
was renamed as Accountant General (Economic and Revenue Sector Audit),

Audit of (i) all transactions from the Consolidated Fund of the State, (ii) all
iransactions relating to the Contingency Fund and Public Accounts

Audit of expenditure

Audit of grants and loans

Audit of Government companies and corporations

Audit of accounts of certain authorities or bodies



Chapter I: Intraduction

Madhya Pradesh from 2 April 2012. After restructuring, audit of accounts of
State Departments/Agencies/Public Sector Undertakings/Autonomous Bodies
grouped under Economic Sector and Revenue Sector is conducted by
Accountant General (Economic and Revenue Sector Audit). The Accountant
General is assisted by four Group officers.

1.5 Planning and conduct of audit

Audit process commences with the assessment of risk of the
department/organisation/autonomous body/scheme, etc. based on expenditure
incurred, criticality/complexity of activities, level of delegated financial
powets, assessment of internal controls and concerns of stakeholders.
Previous audit findings are also considered in this exercise. Based on this risk
assessment, the frequency and extent of audit are decided and an annual audit
plan is formulated to conduct audit on the basis of such risk assessment.
During 2011-12, 10560 party days were used to carry out audit of 781 units
and to conduct one Performance Audit Review and two theme based audit &
& other audit of various departments.

After completion of audit of each unit, Inspection Reports (IRs) containing
audit findings are issued to the head of the unit with a request to furnish
replies within one month of receipt of the IR. Whenever replies are received,
audit findings are either settled or further action for compliance is advised.
Significant audit observations pointed out in these IRs, which require attention
at the highest level in the Government, are processed for inclusion in the Audit
Reports which are submitted to the Governor of the State under Article 151 of
the Constitution of India for causing them to be laid on the table of the State
Legislature.

1.6 _ Significant Audit Observations

In the last few years audit has reported several significant deficiencies in the
implementation of various programmes/activities through performance audits,
as well as on the quality of internal controls in the selected departments.
Similarly, the deficiencies noticed during compliance audits of the
Government departments/organisations have also been reported upon.

1.6.1 Performance Audit and Thematic Audit

The present report contains one performance audit review, and two thematic
paragraphs. The highlights are given in the following paragraphs.

1.6.1.1 Development of Roads in Madhya Pradesh

The road works in Madhya Pradesh are executed by the Public Works
Department with the funding of CRF, NABARD and State Budget.

The fund management of works undertaken through CRF was deficient. The
year wise targets were not fixed for effective monitoring of the programmes.
There were instances of unfruitful expenditure without obtaining forest
clearance. Execution of work was deficient as in large number of cases
MORT&H specifications were disregarded. Tenders were accepted on
unworkable rates resulting in slow progress of works under NABARD.

(Paragraph 2.1)



Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2012

1.6.2 Thematic Paras
1.6.2.1 Construction of minor irrigation schemes in Wainganga Basin

Wainganga Basin of the Water Resources Department covers 1.62 lakh ha area
through 778 MI schemes up to end of 2011-12. The basin covers eight
districts, namely Balaghat, Chhindwara, Dindori, Jabalpur, Katni, Mandla,
Narsinghpur and Seoni and consists of 12 water resources divisions.

The thematic study of construction of minor irrigation schemes in Wainganga
Basin revealed that construction of minor Irrigation schemes was affected due
to inadequate survey, non-synchronisation of process of land acquisition with
tendering process and award of work in mid-way of acquisition of land.
Performance of Lift Irrigation Schemes was poor due to lack of participation
of users. Overlapping of command area of Minor Irrigation schemes with
other Irrigation Schemes was not identified within time resulting in wasteful
expenditure. Monitoring and quality control mechanism were deficient as
basic records were not maintained and inadequate samples were being taken
for testing.

(Paragraph 2.2)
1.6.2.2 Distribution Network of Rani Avanti Bai Lodhi Sagar Project
Rani Avanti Bai Lodhi Sagar (RABLS) Project was initiated with the
objective to imigate 1.57 lakh hectare through Left Bank Canal (LBC).
Construction of 135.50 km long left bank main canal along with its 1915.04
km distribution network was taken up in 1982 at a cost of F 251.85 crore and
was to be completed in 1990. The project was still incomplete after spending
1099.66 crore as of March 2012. Lrrigation potential of 1.23 lakh hectare was
created till January 2012 against the target of 1.57 lakh hectare.

Though the Authority had no financial constraints between 1996-97 and 2009-
10, the canal construction work could not be completed within the targeted
date due to delay in award of work, delay in acquisition of land and slow
progress of works. The RABLS project rescheduled to be completed by 2015
has not achieved the desired objectives of providing irrigation due to non-
synchronisation of work of distribution system and field channels with the
main canal, as only 29,721 ha out of the created IP of 1.23 lakh ha was utilised
as of March 2012. Abnormal delay in execution of main canal from km 130.67
to km 135.50 and its distribution network would be a major bottleneck in
achieving the objective of irrigating 34,382 ha in Narsinghpur district
Contract management at the field level was defective and as a result, progress
was not achieved as targeted.

(Paragraph 2.3)

1.6.3 Department Centric Audit

This report contains department centric audit on Narmada Valley
Development Department. The highlights are given in the following
paragraphs:

NVDA incurred expenditure of हैं 5472.40 crore against allotment of
& 6014.88 crore for creation of infrastructure to utillise Narmada water during
2007-12. There were delays in approval of DPRs from CWC, obtaining



Chapter I: Introduction

environment clearances and award of work. There were further slippages in
execution of dams as well as canals. So far, 11 dams for 13 major projects
across the Narmada and its tributaries for storage of 9.114 MAF water and
canal system for utilisation of 3.157 MAF water only could be completed.

NVDA could not complete canal network of any major project during 2007-
12, Ineffective monitoring, non-preparation of alternative plans and award of
works to ineligible contractors resulted in delays in construction. Assessment
of requirement of fund for completion of the remaining projects was also
inadequate.

NVDA may be able to complete construction of remaining dams and canal
works of major projects by the review year only if works are awarded and
taken up immediately and execution of works is monitored meticulously.
Further, remaining medium projects may be completed by the review year if
all the medium projects are identified immediately, clearances are obtained in
minimum time, funds required for the projects are tied up and other activities
are meticulously planned, monitored and executed.

Audit analysis revealed that preparedness for tapping and utilisation of
Narmada water as per NWDT award is tardy as present progress was not

found satisfactory. Effective action would be required to achieve the targets,
else MP will be lagging behind in getting its due share of Narmada water.

(Paragraph 3.1)
1.6.4 Audit of transactions

Audit has also reported on several significant deficiencies in critical areas
which impact the effective functioning of the Government departments/
organisation. These are broadly categorised and grouped as:

> Non-compliance with rules.

> Expenditure without propriety.

> Persistent and pervasive irregularities
> Failure of oversight/ governance.
1.6.4.1 Non-Compliance with rules

For sound financial administration and financial control, it is essential that
expenditure conforms to financial rules, regulations and orders issued by the
competent authority. This not only prevents irregularities, misappropriation
and frauds, but helps in maintaining good financial discipline. This report
contains instances of non-compliance with rules involving = 36.79 crore
Some significant audit findings are as under:

> Belated deposit of € 34.97 crore towards forest development cess by MP
Forest Produce (Trade & Development) Co-operative Federation Ltd. in
violation of rules resulted in loss of interest of © 4.94 crore to the
Government.

(Paragraph 4.1.1)



Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2012

> Khair wood was auctioned below upset price resulting in loss of revenue

of © 13.82 lakh.
(Paragraph 4.1.2)

> The department spent % 2.17 crore on promotion of Eco-tourism in forest
area in violation of Forest (Conservation) Act, 1980 and without
obtaining approval from Central Government.

(Paragraph 4.1.3)

> Amounts allocated for Joint Forest Management Committee in the budget
were drawn and kept in bank accounts instead of in Personal Deposit
Account in violation of MP Financial Code.

(Paragraph 4.1.4)

> Amount of 1.32 crore was paid to DGS&D for procurement of wireless
sets and peripherals without obtaining license under Indian Telegraph
Act, 1885 and returned amount was kept in Personal Deposit Account of
DFO instead of remitting to the Government account.

(Paragraph 4.1.5)

> The component of ‘open trough’ costing % 48.32 crore was deleted from
the scope of an on-going composite contract. After absorbing an amount
of %21.15 crore in substituted items of work, the resultant cost saving of
% 27.17 crore was also paid by increasing the rates of other items in the
work instead of reducing the over all contract price.

(Paragraph 4.1.6)

> Recovery of हैं 1.05 crore was not made for the excavated hard rock
required in four canal construction works.

(Paragraph 4.1.7)
1.6.4.2 Expenditure without propriety

Authorisation of expenditure from public funds is to be guided by the
principles of propriety and efficiency of public expenditure. Authorities
empowered to incur expenditure are expected to enforce the same vigilance as
a person of ordinary prudence would exercise in respect of his own money and
should enforce financial order and strict economy at every step. Audit has
detected instances of impropriety, extra and infructuous expenditure, some of
which are mentioned below:
> A contractor showing skewed progress of work was sanctioned cash
incentive of € 11.68 crore (paid % 5.84 crore) for early achievement of
financial milestones despite failure to achieve the specified physical
milestones and continued delayed execution.

(Paragraph 4.2.1)



Chapter I: Intraduction

Excess payment of &% 85.59 lakh to a contractor towards price adjustment
towards price adjustment due to adoption of incorrect ratio of Petrol Oil
Lubricant and Material component.

(Paragraph 4.2.2)

> Delay in sanction and execution of a 35 kilometer road under Twelfth
Finance Commission grants resulted in abandoning the work mid way due
to expiry of scheme period and stoppage of funds, and consequential
nugatory expenditure of सं 4.94 crore.

(Paragraph 4.2.3)

The total command area of 1628 hectares of Rajapur Lift Irrigation
Scheme in Rewa district, taken up in 2007, overlapped with the command
area of another major Lift Irrigation Scheme at Teonthar resulting in
wasteful expenditure of रैं 1,63 crore.

(Paragraph 4.2.4)
1.6.4.3 Persistent and pervasive irregularities

An irregularity is considered persistent if it occurs year after year and it
becomes pervasive, when it is prevailing in the entire system. Reoccurrence
of irregularities despite being pointed out in earlier audits, is not only
indicative of non-seriousness on the part of the Executive but is also an
indication of lack of effective monitoring. This, in turn, encourages willful
deviations from observance of rules/regulations and results in weakness of the
administrative structure. Cases of persistence irregularity reported in audit are
discussed below:

> Additional Security Deposit of रैं 3.56 crore were not deducted from
contractors against the unbalance rates.

(Paragraph 4.3.1)

> Extra payment of रैं 9.33 crore on account of escalation was made to
contractors by adopting revised factor for price escalation prior to
approval of the Government

(Paragraph 4.3.2)

1.6.4.4 Failure of oversight/governance

The Government has an obligation to improve the quality of life of the people
through fulfillment of certain goals in the area of health, education,
development and upgradation of infrastructure and public service. However,
Audit scrutiny revealed instances wherein the funds released by the
Government for creating public assets for the benefit of the community
remained unutilised/blocked and/or proved unfruitful/unproductive due to
indecisiveness, lack of administrative oversight and concerted action at
various levels, A few such cases have been discussed below:

> Undue financial aid to contractors of & 8.48 crore and loss to
Government of 743.92 lakh occurred due to non-deduction of additional
security deposit for unbalanced rate items.



Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2012

Loss to Government © 1.68 crore occurred due to non-recovery of
liquidated damages for delay on part of contractors in execution of seven
works.

(Paragraph 4.4.2)

Excess payment was made to contractor ® 81.71 lakh due to adoption of
higher rates for steel in calculating price adjustment and payment for
work not done.

(Paragraph 4.4.3)

> Deficient planning and non-synchronisation of nalla closure work with
waste weir construction led to idle expenditure of रैं 1.53 crore.

(Paragraph 4.4.4)

Earth work was done without watering and rolling resulted in
substandard work costing 49.59 lakh.

(Paragraph 4.4.5)

Increase in quantities of item of work due to improper survey and
estimation resulted in extra cost of © 57.55 lakh.

(Paragraph 4.4.6)

1.7__ Lack of responsiveness of Government to Audit

1.7.1 Inspection reports outstanding

The Heads of Offices and next higher authorities are required to comply with
the observations contained in the Inspection Reports (IRs), promptly rectify
the defects/omissions and report their compliance to the Accountant General
Economic & Revenue Sector Audit (AG) within four weeks of their receipt.
The AG also bring serious irregularities to the notice of the Heads of
Departments

6 Forest, Public Works, Water Resources, Madhya Pradesh Rural Road Development

Authority.



Chapter I: Introduction

As of 30 June 2012, 4831 IRs (19054 paragraphs) were outstanding against
civil departments’. Of these, 2730 IRs (8499 paragraphs) were pending
settlement for more than five years. Year wise details of IRs and paragraphs
outstanding are detailed in Appendix-1,1. Financial rules of the Government
needs to be modified to include provisions and appropriate deterrence to
ensure speedy compliance/response to audit observations

1.7.2. Constitution of Audit Committee

The Government while accepting the recommendations of Shakdhar
Committee (High Powered Committee) constituted a High Power Committee
(May 2000) for monitoring the follow-up action on audit observations. The
Government constituted an Apex Level Committee (April 2009) and 44
Departmental Level Committees (DLCs) for speedy settlement of audit
observations. But no periodicity of meeting of Apex Level Committee was
prescribed in the order. However, departmental level committees are required
to meet once in three months.

While Apex Level State Audit Committee did not meet, only three meetings
of DLC* were held during 2011-12 in the Economic Sector Departments.

1.7.3. Follow up on Audit Reports

As per instructions issued (November 1998) by the Madhya Pradesh Vidhan
Sabha Secretariat, the departments are to intimate the Public Accounts
Committee (PAC) and the PAG/AG about the action taken or any action
proposed by them in respect of Audit Report paragraphs within three months
of presentation of the Audit Report in the State Legislature.

A review of the audit paragraphs pertaining to six Audit Reports disclosed that
departmental replies to 11 paragraphs were still awaited (September 2012) as
per details given in Appendix-1.2.

1.7.4 Paragraphs for discussion by the Public Accounts Committee

Details of paragraphs (excluding General and Statistical) pending discussions
by the Public Accounts Committee as of 30 June 2012 are detailed in
Appendix-1.3.

1.7.5 Government response to PAC’s recommendations

The Chief Secretary, Government of Madhya Pradesh issued (November
1994) instructions to all the departments to inform PAC about the action taken
or the action proposed by them in respect of PAC’s recommendations within
six months of presentation of PAC’s report in the State Legislature. The copies
of action taken notes are also to be endorsed to the PAG/AG for his
comments.

The departments did not furnish ATNs on PAC's recommendations on 300
Audit Report paragraphs (September 2012). ATNs had not been furnished on
the recommendations made on the Audit Reports for the year as early as 1986-
87. Department-wise and year-wise details are given in Appendix-1.4.

1.7.6 Response of the departments to Draft Audit Reports

Dratt Paragraphs and Performance Audit are forwarded to the Principal
Secretary, Finance and Principal Secretaries of different departments demi-
officially seeking confirmation of facts and figures and their comments
thereon within a period of six weeks. Out of 19 Draft Paragraphs, one
Performance Audit Reports, two Thematic paras and one department centric
audit forwarded to the various departments between June and October 2012,
the Government had replied to only 10 draft paragraphs.

2.1 Performance Audit on Development of roads in Madhya Pradesh

Highlights

= Government of India, Ministry of Road Transport and Highways (Department
otf Roads and Transport) approved 298 works of Major District Roads and Other
District Roads in Madhya Pradesh, between 2001 and 2012! for widening,
strengthening and upgradation at a cost of 2224.78 crore; out of which 152 works
were sanctioned during 2007-08 to 2011-12 at a cost of = 1653.67 crore. During
2007-08 to 2011-12, the Government of Madhya Pradesh approved 510 works of
village roads for construction and improvement at a cost of = 1499 crore from
National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development (NABARD) loan assistance.
Government had also undertaken the development and maintenance of roads
through the State Budgetary Provision under plan and non-plan heads on which, an
expenditure of = 1109.86 crore was made during the review period. Between 2007-
08 and 2011-12, 42 works under Central Road Fund (CRF) and 169 works under
NABARD were actually completed. Huge shortfall in achievement of planned
connectivity was noticed under the CRF/ NABARD schemes, though there was no
financial constraint.

Major deficiencies noticed during audit are mentioned below:

> There were instances of misutilisation of CRF amounting to € 12.34 crore.
(Paragraph 2.1.6.1 and 2.1.13.4)
> There were abnomnal delays in completion of sanctioned roads under CRF/

NABARD. Premature upgradation of roads and award of works without
clearance from Forest Department resulted in wasteful/unfruitful
expenditure of हैं 15.19 crore and extra cost of € 5.69 crore.
(Paragraph 2.1.8.1 and 2.1.8.2)
> Misclassification of work of strengthening as renewal work led to extra cost
of € 2.30 crore besides violation of the orders of Finance Department.
(Paragraph 2.1.9.2)
> Unwatranted provision of Surface Dressing (SD) on Water Bound
Macadam (WBM) resulted in extra cost of € 2.76 crore.
(Paragraph 2.1.9.3)
> Short levy of liquidated damages (LD), ineligible payment of price variation
and instances of loss to Government amounting 10 हैं 25.37 crore were
noticed.
(Paragraph 2.1.10.1, 2.1. 10.2 and 2.1.10.3)
> There were instances of short recovery of mobilisation advance, undue
financial aid and irregular sanction of secured advances.
(Paragraph 2.1.10.4 and 2.1.10.5)

2.1.2 Organisational set-up

The PWD, an implementing agency for taking up road works with the funding
of CRF, NABARD and State budget, is headed by Principal Secretary (PS) at
the Government level. Engineer-in-Chief (E-in-C) is the technical head of the
department, who is assisted by seven Chief Engineers (CEs) in zones. There
are 14 Superintending Engineers (SEs) at the circle level as detailed in the
organogram. The SEs are assisted by 53 divisions, each headed by an
Executive Engineer (EE) for execution of roads and building works.

2.1.3 Audit objectives

The audit was conducted with a view to assess whether:

> financial management was effective and adequate control existed for
management and utilisation of funds;

> road works were well planned and executed according to the milestones,
standard designs and specifications of Ministry of Road Transport and
highways (MORT&H) and Indian Road Congress (IRC)* with due
attention to efficacy and economy; and

State Highways (SH) and Major District Roads (MDRs). widening of two lanes to four
lanes and stengthening of weak pavement sections ete.

Specifications of MORT&EH and [RC were applicable for execution of roads under CRE,
NABARD and Siate Budget.



Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2012

> contract management and quality control monitoring mechanism were
effective.

2.1.4 Audit criteria

The audit findings were based on criteria drawn from:

> Provisions of the Works Department Manual (WD Manual), specifications
of Indian Road Congress (IRC), Financial Rules, instructions issued by
the Government of India (GOI), State Government and MORT&H,
CRE Act 2000 and Rules 2007, and
NABARD guidelines,

Approved designs and specifications as prescribed in the Detailed Project
Report (DPR) for construction of roads and

> Terms and conditions of contracts.

2.1.5 Scope and methodology of audit

The development of road works was being undertaken through 53 divisions of
Public Works Department (Building and Road) under seven Zones (headed by
CEs) and 14 circles (headed by SEs). For the performance audit, 16 divisions”
were selected. Statistical information and data were collected from the E-in- C
office. In the performance audit, transactions for the period 2007-08 to 2011-
12 relating to planning, implementation and execution of 120 road works in
Madhya Pradesh undertaken with the assistance of CRF, and NABARD, were
examined during November 2011 to August 2012

The audit objectives, criteria and methodology were discussed with the
Secretary, PWD during the entry conference held in January 2012. In the exit
conference held in September 2012 with the Principal Seecretary, PWD, audit
findings were discussed and the response of the Department elicited. The draft
Performance Audit Report was issued to the Department (September 2012),
the reply has not been received so far (March 2013) .

2.1.6 Fund Management

Under Section 7 of the CRF Act, funds are disbursed for projects, schemes or
activities relating to the construction and development of State roads and
routed through the State Budget. NABARD ‘ases the sanctioned amount of
loan for road works on reimbursement basis. NABARD may also release
funds as advances according to the norms applicable from time to time, if the
State Government so desires.

During the period 2007-2008 to 2011-2012, an expenditure of = 996.90 crore
and % 1741.09 crore was incurred utilising the CRF and NABARD funds,
respectively. Besides, the State Government also provided funds of = 1232.17

crore from State Plan Budget for development and maintenance of existing

2.1.6.1 Misutilisation of Central Road Fund

According to the guidelines issued® (May 1984) by MORT&H for the work
under CRF, no work beyond sanctioned estimates leading to increase in scope
of work or change in the specification, should be undertaken without the prior
written approval from MORT&H. Further, according to the provision of WD
manual anticipated or actual saving on sanctioned estimates for a definite
project may not, without the sanctioning authority, be applied to additional
work which was not included in the original scope of work.

We noticed (April 2012 to May 2012) that supplementary schedule for
additional work of Maksudangarh bypass road (Guna) for हैं 3.10 crore and
Uchehra bypass Road (Satna) for = 4.10 crore were sanctioned by the Chief
Engineer (North Zone) and Chief Engineer (Rewa Zone), respectively without
approval from MORT&H and without inviting tenders for the supplementary
works.

The E-in-C during exit conference stated (September 2012) that looking to the
lower tender percentage, the additional works were taken up with due consent
of MORT&H.

The reply of E-in-C is not convincing as the works were not included in the
administrative approval sanctioned by MORT&H and any change in the
original scope of work required prior written approval from the MORT&H.
No evidence of prior written approval from MORT&H was produced to Audit.

Letter no. RW/NH11/coord/32/84 dated 19.05 1984.

2.1.7 Physical targets and achievement

Implementation of the programmes is required to be monitored by the E-in-C
and the CEs of the zones so as to achieve the targets within the specified time
and cost and realisation of benefits expected from the programmes

We observed that though financial and physical status of the works was
prepared by the E-in-C, year wise targets were not fixed for effective
monitoring of the programmes and timely completion of projects. The
completion of the works was delayed as discussed in the following paragraphs.

under CRF, release of funds was tied to the submission of utilisation
certificates and physical progress of the work. Besides, the fact that the
department had over % 309.35 crore at the end of March 2011 and % 253.51
crore remaining in the fund as at the end of March 2012, indicated that release
of fund had no impact on the progress of work.

2.1.8 Planning and Project Management

Road construction requires correct planning, construction methods, execution
and implementation of critical safety standards. Since the main reasons for
delays in overall execution and cost overruns are delays in land acquisition,
forest clearance and planning inaccuracies, project and logistics planning can
help in timely execution of projects and avoidance of cost overruns. For every
project, detailed survey and investigation are required for proper planning and
correct estimation before awarding the work for execution.

The issues relating to the deficiencies in planning are discussed below:

2.1.8.1 Improper planning for development of roads

The guidelines of CRF provide that reads where improvement work has been
undertaken in last three years would not be eligible to be considered for
sanctioning. Further, the TRC’ specifications provide that renewal work
should be undertaken at the rate of 20 per cent of the constructed roads length
per year. Besides, the terms and conditions of contracts provide that
contractors are responsible for repair and maintenance of roads till the
performance guarantee period (18 months after completion of works).

Under State Road Improvement Programme (SRIP), seven roads in three
divisions* were renewed in total 92.00 kms length during January 2008 to
November 2008. The roads were under performance guarantee for the period
up to November 2009 to September 2010. Nevertheless, these roads were
prematurely reselected (between May 2008 and August 2009) for widening
and strengthening work under CRF and NABARD. As a result, the work of
renewal of roads (with bituminous course) valued at हैं 6.34 crore executed
earlier proved infructuous as detailed in the Appendix 2.1. This is indicative
of the fact that there was no long term planning to avoid selection of roads for
development as even the works not due for renewal were selected for
upgradation resulting in wasteful expenditure thereon.

The E-in-C during exit conference stated (September 2012) that only 20 mm
to 25 mm thin layer of bituminous course was executed to maintain the riding
quality of the roads and there was no improvement work on these roads. The
Principal Secretary, PWD agreed to look in to the matter.

The reply of the E-in-C is not acceptable because works executed were under
improvement plan of SRIP using bituminous renewal

2.1.8.2 Deficient planning in awarding the works without clearance from
Forest Department

According to the order (June 2000) of the Government of India, Ministry of
Forest and in terms of the provisions of the Forest (Conservation) Act, 1980,
it is necessary to obtain clearance of land from the Forest Department before
taking up the work of upgradation of roads in forest areas. According to the
provision of IRC, one of the primary considerations in the location and design
of a road is that the overall cost of transportation of material is the minimum.
For satisfying this consideration, detailed survey and investigation should be
undertaken to determine the most desirable alignment. All the operations
involved should be planned in advance in a logical sequence. Further, in order
to mitigate adverse impact on the environment, it is imperative to consider the
environmental impact of Highway projects also at all stages including the
planning stage.

Instances of deficient planning in awarding of works without obtaining
clearance for forest land resulting in unfruitful expenditure are discussed in the
succeeding paragraphs:

> Two works of roads in PWD Division 2 Gwalior, were taken up (April
2005/ November 2007) irregularly for construction without obtaining
clearance from Forest Department though there were involvements of
Sonchiraya sanctuary in the roads alignment. The department had to
foreclose these works in July 2009/ September 2009 in terms of the
agreements after incurring expenditure of & 3.35 crore as clearances for
the forest land (Sonchiraya sanctuary) included in the alignment of the
two roads, could not be obtained from the Forest Department. This

[1.9Executionofwork —————~Y Execution of work

All the works executed under CRF requires approval from MORT&H and the
scope of work with respect to specifications was not to be changed during
execution without the prior approval of the MORT&H. In the case of the
works executed under NABARD funding, the terms and conditions of the
agreement require that the works are executed strictly as per MORT&H
specifications. It however provides that if any changes in the specifications
become necessary due to site condition, they should be incorporated in the
DPR” for the approval of the Government. According to the specifications of
MORT&H and pocket book for Highway Engineers (IRC), Flexible
Pavements for new roads should be designed in accordance with IRC: 37
Guidelines for the design of Flexible Pavement.

sub-grade strength in terms of California Bearing Ratio (CBR), Traffic count,

The issues relating to the deficiencies in the designing and consequential extra
cost are discussed below:

> Extra cost due to unwarranted provision of richer specification

According to the provision of IRC: 37, where the designed traffic is less than
one Million Standard Axles" (MSA) and sub-grade'* CBR! of more than five
per cent, provision of 20 mm Open Grade Premix Carpet (OGPC) with seal
coat should be provided as a bituminous course.

We observed (February 2012 to August 2012) in 13 test checked works of
roads'® in eight divisions!” that the estimates of the roads were erroneously
prepared. Though the sub-grade was prepared with the soil having CBR more
than five per cent and designed traffic intensity was less than one MSA,
Bituminous Macadam (BM) and Semi Dense Bituminous Macadam (SDBC)
were provided and executed instead of OGPC and Seal coat. This led to
avoidable extra cost of € 11.50 crore as detailed in Appendix 2.2.

The E-in-C during exit conference stated (September 2012) that specifications
were only guidelines and Engineer in Charge, having technical expertise, were
empowered to deviate from the specifications according to site conditions.

The reply is not acceptable as the MORT&Hs technical instruction provides

that the specifications of work will not be changed during execution without
prior approval of the Ministry. Besides, all the parameters of site condition
which determine the design of the flexible pavement had been considered in
IRC specifications. Therefore, adoption of a higher specification due to faulty
estimation resulted in extra cost which was avoidable.

> Excess execution of Granular Sub-Base (GSB) beyond the scope of
specifications and DPR

The choice and thickness of GSB is decided on the basis of specifications
prescribed in IRC: 37, 2001. The thickness of GSB is based on the sub-grade
strength in terms of CBR and projected traffic.

We observed (December 2011 - July 2012) in nine test checked works of roads
in seven divisions'* that traftic intensity were wrongly calculated by adopting
initial traffic count different from those obtained during survey, using
increased lane distribution factor than those prescribed and incorrect
determination of the strength of subgrade!’ in respect of four works”’. As a
result, thickness of GSB provided was in excess of the required thickness in

terms of IRC 37. In other five works, execution of GSB was in excess of the

The EEs replied (December 2011 - July 2012) that the execution of GSB was

neous computation of traffic intensity which resulted in unwarranted
on and execution of excess thickness of GSB. Besides, no specific reply
was given regarding execution of GSB in thickness more than that provided in
the DPRs in respect of the five works.

> Extra cost due to unnecessary widening of road width

According to guidelines issued (December 1999) by the MORT&H and
circular issued (August 2010) by E-in-C, the traffic that should be considered
for design improvement of road should be based on expected volume of traffic
at the end of design life with the specified traffic growth rate. The volume of
traffic is to be calculated in terms of numbers of passenger car units for the
purpose of determining the width of the road. The capacity of road that could
cater the traffic for the design life is as detailed below:

(i) Traffic volume up to 4000 PCU” single lane having formation width
9.00 metre and carriage width 3.75 metre.

(ii) Traffic volume above 4000 PCU up to 10000 PCU - intermediate lane
having formation width 10 metre and carriage width 5.50 metre.

(iii) Traffic volume above 10000 PCU doub le lane having formation width
10 metre and carriage width 7.0 metre.

We noticed (August 2012) that in case of improvement and up-gradation of
five road works in two divisions” the projected traffic volume was computed
considering MSA as the factor instead of adopting PCUs as prescribed in the
MORT&H guidelines. Adoption of MSA as a factor was prescribed only to
determine the thickness of the crust. Due to adoption of MSA instead of PCUs
for determining the traffic volume, the roads were constructed in carriage
width of 5.5 metre and 7.0 metre against the required carriage width of 3.75
metre and 5.5 metre, respectively in terms of the projected traffic (designed
traffic) on these roads. This resulted in extra cost of = 8.16 crore as detailed in
Appendix 2.4.

The EEs replied (August 2012) that the widening of roads were as per the
sanctioned estimates.

The replies are not acceptable as sanction for the estimates were obtained
based on incorrect computation of projected traffic volume, which had resulted
in extra cost.

» Inadequate provision of bituminous base course

According to TRC: 37, 2001, application of Bituminous Macadam (BM) base
course may desirably be restricted only to the roads designed to carry traffic
less than five million standard axle (MSA). Dense Bituminous Macadam
(DBM) binder course is recommended for roads designed to carry more than
five MSA. It also provides that for traffic of more than five MSA and sub
grade strength of more than five per cent, 78 mm BM is required and for
traffic of 3 MSA, 50 mm BM is required. According to the specifications, 55
mm DBM can be taken as equivalent to 78 mm of BM.

We observed (April 2012 to May 2012) in test check of records of three roads
in three divisions that provision of BM base course in the DPR was not
adequate in terms of the IRC 37, 2001 specifications as detailed in the table
below;

according to the requirements of specification. The argument of site conditions
can only be advanced when the DPR provides richer specifications than those
prescribed in the guidelines

2.1.9.3 Unwarranted execution of Surface Dressing and Intermediate Tack
coat
According to the construction procedure stipulated in the specifications”*,
during execution of the work, the contractor should provide and maintain a
passage for traffic cither along a part of the existing carriageway under
improvement or along a temporary diversion constructed close to the highway.
All the arrangement like construction of temporary diversion or execution of
single coat surface dressing (SD) and intermediate tack coat (prior to
execution of semi dense bituminous concrete on BM) for traffic during
construction are considered incidental to the works and was the responsibility
of the contractor.
Unwarranted > We observed (November 2011 to August 2012) that 27 contractors
even of executed SD in between WBM and Bituminous Macadam surfaces (BM)
dressing in 10 works under CRF in six divisions*’, 13 works under NABARD in
beyond the six divisions and four works under State Plan in four divisions, to allow
scope of traffic over freshly laid WBM and were paid for that. Since the execution
पाक एक of surface dressing for traffic arrangement during construction was
cost 2.76 incidental to the works, the contractors should not have been paid for
crore.

deployment of Electronic Sensor Paver, deduction at the rates of ¥ 150 per cu
m and हैं 350 per cu m from the rates of DLC and CC M40 grade respectively,
should be made from the bills of contractor against the quantities executed.
We observed (April 2012 to May 2012) in respect of four road works in three
divisions that the contractors were neither having Paver with Electronic
Sensor nor had they deployed the machinery through lease. Hence, deductions
at prescribed rates were to be made from the contractors, which were not done
by the department. This resulted in excess payment of हैं 73.65 lakh to the
contractors as detailed in Appendix 2.7.

The EEs Satna and Narsinghpur stated (May 2012) that Paver with Electronic
Sensor was deployed by the contractors.

The replies of EEs Satna and Narsinghpur are not acceptable as Paver with
Electronic Sensor neither existed in the list of plant and machinery submitted
by the contractors in technical bids nor any lease certificates produced in

2.1.9.5 Excess payment to the contractor due to non-deployment of
technical expertise

According to the provisions of Standard Bidding Documents (SBD) vide
Annexure 1, six graduate Engineers were required for the execution of work
valued more than % five crore. In the event of non-deployment/short
deployment of technical expertise, recovery at the rate of € 10000 per graduate
Engineer or हैं 5000 per diploma holder Engineer per month was to be made.
We observed (February 2012 to June 2012) during scrutiny of eight road
works in five divisions” that the contractors neither deployed adequate
technical expertise as required in terms of the provisions of contract nor did
the Department recover the amount from the bills of contractors on account of
inadequate deployment of technical experts. This not only entailed excess
payment of हैं 74.70 lakh as detailed in Appendix 2.8, but also exposed the
department to the risk of execution of sub-standard works.

The EEs stated (February 2012 to June 2012) that adequate technical staff
were deployed by the contractors.

The replies are not acceptable as the statements submitted by the contractors
indicated deployment of less technical staff than mentioned in the contracts.

2.1.10 Contract Management

Contract management is the process of systematically and efficiently
managing contract creation, execution and analysis for the purpose of
maximising financial and operational performance and minimising risk. The
contracts of road works were executed on item rate and percentage rate basis.
Shortcomings noticed in contract management are discussed in succeeding
paragraphs.

21101 Short recovery of Liquidated Damages (LD)

According to the contractual provisions, the time allowed as entered in the
agreement should be strictly observed by the contractor and shall be reckoned
from the date of work order. The terms and conditions of agreements further
provides that LD at the rate 1/16 per cent of value of works per week limited
to 6 per cent of contract price for delay in works should be recovered from the
contractor under the contracts on percentage rate tender*”. In the case of item
rate contract*!, if the contractor fails to comply with the time for completion as
stipulated in the contract, the contractor shall pay to the employer at the rate
1/2000" of initial contract price per day as LD for such default subject to a
maximum of 10 per cent of contract price.

2.1.10.2 Ineligible payment of price variation

The instruction issued (March 2007) by E-in-C, appended as additional special
conditions of contract lays down that any variation (plus/minus) in the cost of
bitumen/steel will be paid or deducted on the theoretical consumption of
bitumen/steel. The difference in price would be worked out for the agreement
period only.

We noticed (December 2011 to May 2012) in eight agreements under four
divisions™, that the contractors executed bituminous work after the agreement
period, Nevertheless, price variation was paid to the contractors. This resulted
in ineligible payment of हैं 2.47 crore to the contractors as detailed in
Appendix 2.10.

The E-in-C during exit conference stated (September 2012) that the
contractual provision would have to be amended to facilitate payment of price
variation, where necessary.

The reply does not give any reason for payment of price variation for period
beyond the agreement period in these works in violation of the existing
contract conditions.

executed work amounting to % 5.68 crore. Due to paucity of fund, the

crore (based on new SOR) under CRF through the funding of Annual Repair.

AA resulted in extra cost of € 1.73 crore”.

strengthening and upgradation at cost more than the sanctioned cost and
consequential termination of the work.

2.1.10.4 Non-recovery of mobilisation advance from contractors

According to Clause 51 of Standard Bidding Document for item rate contract,
the employer shall make advance payment to the contractor at the rate of 10
per cent of contract price against an unconditional Bank Guarantee. The terms
and conditions of contract further provide that the advance loan shall be repaid
with percentage deductions from the interim payments certified by the
Engineer under the contract. Deductions shall commence in the next Interim
Payment Certificate following that in which the total of all such payments to
the contractor has reached not less than 20 per cent of the contract price or six
months from the date of payment of first installment of advance, whichever
period concludes earlier, and shall be made at the rate of 20 per cent of the
amounts of all Interim Payment Certificates until such time the loan is repaid.
It is also provided that the loan shall be completely repaid prior to the expiry
of the original time for completion.

We observed (December 2011 to August 2012 ) in test check of three road
works in three divisions** that an amount of % 2.14 crore of mobilisation
advance remained unrecovered even though the entire stipulated period of
completion of works exp between May 2011 and December 2011. This
resulted in undue financial aid to the contractor of = 2.14 crore as detailed in
Appendix 2.11.

The Principal Secretary PWD and E-in-C during exit conference (September
2012) stated that advances had already been recovered from the contractors.

The reply is not acceptable as the entire amount of mobilisation advance was
not recovered before the expiry of the scheduled completion period in terms of
the contracts.

2.1.10.5 Irregular refund of performance security deposit

According to additional special conditions appended with agreements,
performance security deposit (PSD) to be taken for the due performance and
maintenance of the contract under the terms and conditions of contract, will be
the earnest money plus a deduction of 10 per cent from the payment made in
the running bills. Fifty per cent of the PSD shall be released to the contractor
after two years from the date of completion. Balance 50 per cent shall be
released to the contractor after three years from the date of completion.

We observed (December 2011 to April 2012) that the performance security
deposit (PSD) of % 83.42 lakh was refunded to the contractor prior to the

against any specific recovery. The EE Sagar stated (April 2012) that the PSD
was refunded by the then EE. The EE Gwalior stated (March 2012) that PSD
was released as per rule.

The replies of EEs are not acceptable as the PSDs were refunded to the
contractors before the expiry of the defect liability period mentioned in the
contract.

paid only on imperishable material brought to the site by the contractor for its
use in the respective work. The advance shall be paid at the rate of 75 per cent
of the amount mentioned in the invoice.

We observed (February 2012 to June 2012) during the scrutiny of two roads in
two divisions that secured advance was paid to the contractor on perishable
material like 4 mm metal*? and VG 30 (Bitumen) which were brought to the

2.1.10.7 Slow progress of work under NABARD

According to the terms and conditions of sanctions of loan by NABARD, once
a particular project is cleared, loans are released in installments usually on
reimbursement basis by the Regional Offices of the NABARD. Payment by
NABARD depends on the progress of the project and utilisation of funds. The
terms and conditions of contracts for works provided that the time specified
for completion of work was the essence of contract. Monthly progress reports
of works are sent by the divisional authority to CE and E-in-C for monitoring
purpose. The progress reports sent by the divisional offices are compiled at
Office of the E-in-C at monthly basis. Finance Department (FD) of the State
Government would continue to be the nodal department for documentation,
drawal of fund, etc. Therefore the projects have to be routed through the FD
for sanction under RIDF and watched quarterly during the high power
committee headed by the Chief Secretary, GOMP.

Since the works were being undertaken through NABARD loans, they were
required to be completed in time so as to avoid unnecessary accrual of interest
on NABARD loan and to provide the maximum benefits to the habitations.

We observed between November 2011 and May 2012 during test check of
road works undertaken through NABARD loan during the period 2006-07 to
2009-10, that in respect of 56 works in seven Divisions’’, progress was very
slow and remained incomplete. These works were executed up to Earth Work/
GSB/ WBM level; even after lapse of three to four times of the stipulated
completion period. Bituminous works were neither started by the contractors
nor any penal action taken by the department in terms“! of the contracts. The
tardy progress of works not only led to unfruitful expenditure of हैं 109.54
crore but also created a liability of € 20.39 crore on account of interest payable
to NABARD as detailed in Appendix 2.12. We analysed that one of the main
reason for slow progress of the work was awarding the contract on
unworkable rates which were up to 32.34 per cent below the estimates. Where
the tender percentage was 3.33 per cent below the estimates, the works were
almost completed.

The Principal Secretary, PWD and E-in-C during exit conference (September
2012) stated that these works suffered due to paucity of funds and assured to
improve the progress of the works

The replies are not acceptable as penal action was not taken by the department
for the abnormal delays in completion of works to avoid interest payable to
NABARD on incomplete works.

2.1.10.8 Undue financial aid due to non-obtaining additional security

According to clause 29.5 and 34.1 of Instructions to bidder of contract
documents, if the bid of the successful bidder is seriously unbalanced in
relation to the Engineers estimate of the cost of work, the employer may
require the amount of the performance security to be of a level sufficient to
protect the employer against financial loss in the event of default of the
successful bidder under the contract.

The terms and conditions of contract further provides that the contractor has to
deposit an amount equivalent to 5 per cent of the contract price plus additional
security for unbalanced bids within 21 days of receipt of the letter of
acceptance. The additional security for unbalanced bids shall be held by the
department up to 28 days from the date of issue of the certificate of
completion.

We noticed (July 2012 to August 2012) in respect of three road works in two
divisions® that the contractors quoted disproportionately unbalanced rates in
respect of some of the items against the estimated rates. Nevertheless, the
department did not obtain additional security deposit to meet financial loss in
the event of default by the contractors, This led to undue financial aid to the
contractors of € 2.66 crore as detailed in Appendix 2.13.

The EE PWD, Khandwa stated (July 2012 to August 2012) in replies that the
work is likely to be completed in time, The EE PWD, Chindwara further stated
that 45.00 lakh has been kept in deposit as additional security deposit.

The replies are not acceptable as additional security deposit against the
unbalanced items have not been obtained to the extent prescribed in the
agreements to protect the financial interests of the department.

2.1.11 Quality assurance and monitoring mechanism

According to the provision of IRC SP 11 and contractual provision appended
with the contract, it is the prime responsibility of the Engineer-in-charge to
ensure that the work performed and all the material incorporated in works
conforms to the specifications. Tests for checking if the quality of material is
in accordance with the specifications are also to be conducted at required
frequencies. It is also essential that the results of all quality control tests and
observations are systematically recorded and carefully preserved.

Instances of weak quality control and monitoring mechanism are discussed
below;

° Seriously unbalanced denotes the quoted rates of certain items which are

disproportionately higher than the estimated rates of that particular item plus or
minus overall tender percentage.

2.1.11.1 Execution of Bituminous Macadam work below specifications

The specifications of MORT&H require that the work of Bituminous
Macadam (BM) shall be covered with the wearing course of Semi Dense
Bituminous Concrete (SDBC) within 48 hours. If there was a delay, the BM
shall be covered with a seal coat to be treated as incidental part and not to be
paid to the contractors separately. The specifications further required that
SDBC should be provided on firm bituminous course (BM).

We noticed (November 2011 to July
2012) in 13 road works in seven
Divisions™ that the BM executed in
50368.84 cu m quantity at a cost of
% 17.27 crore were overlaid by the
next wearing course of SDBC after
expiry of 15 days to one year and in
few cases it remained exposed
during the rainy season as seal coat
was not done as detailed in
| View of Basoda Sironj road under Appendix 2.14.

CRF The Principal Secretary PWD and
E-in-C during exit conference (September 2012) stated that due to practical
difficulties in simultaneous execution of BM and SDBC, BM was not covered
immediately with next wearing course.

The replies are not acceptable as in case of any difficulty in timely execution
of SDBC, BM should be covered by seal coat at the cost of the contractor as
the work of seal coat is treated as incidental. If the BM remains exposed
during the rainy season, there remained the possibility of premature slippage
of SDBC (wearing course) even after application of tack coat prior to the
execution of SDBC because of dust deposit over BM course and thereby
weakening the bond between BM and SDBC. We further noticed (May 2012)
that contrary to these specifications, SDBC on 38599.28 sq m of Lalganj-
Mangawan road work under NABARD in Rewa division was provided on
WBM surface resulting in below specification work valued at हैं 45.34 lakh.

The EE stated (May 2012) that the work was done according to site
requirement and traffic intensity.

The reply is not acceptable as specifications and guidelines of MORT&H were
not adhered to.

cubes shall not be less than 10 Mega Pascal (MPa) at seven days of castings.

> Substandard execution of granular sub-base (GSB)

According to the provisions of IRC pocket book for Highway Engineers, sub-
base material should be utilised having CBR more than 20 per cent in case of
traffic intensity up to 2 million standard axles (MSA) and of 30 per cent in
case of traffic intensity more than 2 MSA.

We noticed (February 2012) in respect of the work of Sehore-Bilkisganj-
Bhadbhada road in PWD Division, Schore, that though the traffic intensity on
this road was more than 3 MSA, the GSB utilised on this road was having
CBR of 22 per cent. This resulted in below specification execution of GSB.
We further observed that there was no system of submitting reports to higher
authority in case of execution of work with deviations from the specifications.

The EE PWD Division, Schore stated (February 2012) that the works were
carried out using GSB having CBR value more than 30 per cent.

The reply of EE is not acceptable as the CBR test results depicted that the
GSB utilised in the work was having CBR of 22 per cent only.

2.1.12 Monitoring

According to the departmental instructions, implementation of the programme
was required to be monitored by the E-in-C and the CEs of the Zone for
achieving the targets within time and realisation of full economic benefits. The
guidelines (2007) of CRF provided that project monitoring shall be done at
regular intervals in methodical manner to avoid time and cost overrun.

For execution of projects under NABARD loan, allotment is made in the
budget. Expenditure incurred there against is claimed on monthly basis from
NABARD. Therefore, close monitoring is required for watching progress of
works so as to avoid time and cost overrun as delays result in extea interest
burden on the State Government. Year wise targets are also required to be
fixed so as to enable the higher authority to monitor progress of works vis-a-
vis targets so fixed and thereby ensuring timely completion of projects.

For the purpose of monitoring works, monthly progress reports are sent by the
divisions to the controlling offices (CE and E-in-C).

We noticed from the monthly progress reports being sent by the divisions that
monthly progress reports are not in uniform format across the divisions.
Schedule completion dates, projections of completion of works in case of
delays, fact of approval/ non approval of time extension and reasons for delay
in completion of works are not being mentioned in the reports. In the absence
of these details, proper appreciation of progress of works, problems in
execution and necessary remedial measures may not be adequate. No target of
completion of works was fixed by the department, Only actual financial and
physical progress of the works was being compiled by the E-in-C and reported
to the Chief Secretary GOMP on quarterly basis. In the absence of a target for
completion of works, shortfall, if any, cannot be identified and remedied. We

38


Chapter IT Performance Audit

further noticed that there were delays in completion of works with reference to
schedule completion periods as discussed in paragraph 10.7 supra.

The E-in-C during exit conference stated (September 2012) that length-wise
target was not fixed due to disproportionate release of funds by the MORT&H.
With respect to works under NABARD, it was stated that the programme
suffered due to paucity of funds. He also assured to improve the progress of
the works.

The reply is not convincing as generally there were savings of funds in all the
three categories, viz, CRF, NABARD and State budget as shown in paragraph
6 supra and none of the works suffered due to lack of funds.

and action would be taken accordingly.

2.1.13.4 Irregular expenditure

According to the guidelines issued from time to time and in August 2010 by
E-in-C, PWD and MORT&H circular (May 1984) for execution of CRF
works, the items of road furniture®! must be included in the scope of work. No
work beyond the scope of sanctioned estimate should be undertaken without
obtaining prior written approval of the MORT&H.

We noticed (April 2012 to June 2012) that in seven road works” under
CRF in PWD Divisions, Guna and Dewas, items like road marking,
speed bump, road studs, cats eye and sign boards etc. costing हैं 5.14
crore were purchased directly through the open market without
obtaining prior written approval of MORT&H. These items were
shown utilised by the department by directly charging to the work. We
also noticed that in two road work** under PWD Division, Dewas,
though the road furniture items were included in the scope of work and
also executed by the contractors, road furniture costing हैं 1.06 crore
was again charged on these two works by making transfer entry from
another road work viz. Rajoda-Kelod-Double Chowki. Though the
transfer entry once the material was directly charged on work was not
allowed in terms of the provisions of MPWD Manual. As the road
furniture on the above two works had already been executed, the
possibility of fictitious accounting by further transfer of road furniture
amounting to हैं 1.06 crore cannot be ruled out in Audit.

The E-in-C stated (September 2012) during exit conference that the
road furniture works were executed through the saving of CRF, which
was within the amount of administrative approval. The Principal
Secretary, PWD assured to look into the matter of transfer entry.

The reply is not acceptable as prior approval for undertaking work beyond the
scope of sanctioned estimates was not obtained from the MORT&H.

2.1.14 Conclusion

The fund management of works undertaken through CRF was deficient and
there were instances of misutilisation of funds as works beyond the approved
scope of work were executed. The year wise targets were not fixed for
effective monitoring of the programmes and timely completion of projects.
There were instances of unfruitful expenditure due to incorrect selection of
road alignment and awarding of works without obtaining forest clearance.
Execution of work was deficient as in large number of cases MORT&H
specifications were disregarded during preparation and approval of estimates.
Works were awarded without invitation of tenders and liquidated damages
were not recovered though the delays were attributable to contractors. Tenders
were accepted on abnonnally unworkable rates resulting in slow progress of
works under NABARD. The stipulated mobilisation advances were not
recovered from the contractor in time.

2.1.15 Recommendations

> The progress of the works should be reviewed at periodic and regular
intervals at various levels so as to identify bottlenecks and ensure timely
completion.

> The work should be taken up after obtaining all the necessary clearance

from the concerned authority.

Contractual provisions should be enforced in case of withdrawal/

termination of the contract to safeguard the interest of the Government.

Contract management needs further strengthening in ensuring that

payments are made according to progress of work and as provided in the

contracts.

> A well structured system for internal control and monitoring of works
should be put in place.

Water Resources Department

2.2 Thematic paragraph on construction of minor irrigation
schemes in Wainganga Basin

2.2.1 Introduction

Wainganga Basin of the Department covers 1.62 lakh ha area through 778 MI
schemes up to end of 2011-12. The basin covers eight districts, namely
Balaghat, Chhindwara, Dindori, Jabalpur, Katni, Mandla, Narsinghpur and
Seoni and co of 12 water resources divisions headed by Executive
Engineers (EEs) under administrative control of four Superintending
Engineers (SEs). CE Wainganga Basin is responsible for administrative
control over works under the Basin. Sub Divisional Officers (SDOs), who are
under the control of EE of Division, are responsible for preparation of new
schemes.

2.2.2 Audit Scope and methodology

The thematic study covered transactions relating to construction and
maintenance of Minor Irrigation (MI) schemes* of the Wainganga Basin
during the period 2007-12. During audit, records relating to planning and
construction of MI schemes, utilisation of created irrigation potential and
utilisation of created infrastructure of MI schemes were scrutinised. Out of the
12 divisional offices in the Wainganga Basin, Audit examined in detail the
records of randomly selected six divisional offices and office of the Chief

The surtace and ground water irrigation schemes having cultivable command area up
to 2000 hectare (ha) are classified as minor irrigation (MI) schemes.

3



Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2012

Engineer, Wainganga Basin, Seoni between December 2011 and May 2012
and information has been collected from remaining six divisional offices.

Entry conference with the executives of the Department was held in October
2011. Exit conference was held in September 2012 with the Principal
Secretary (PS) to discuss the audit findings and replies of the Department are
incorporated at suitable places. The reply of E-in-C was received in October
2012.

2.2.3 Planning

The planning process in the context of MT schemes includes identification of
command area to be ittigated, feasibility of project, assessment of site
suitability for head works* as well as canal works*”, formulation of strategies
to achieve them, atrangement of the means required, and implementation,
direction and monitoring of all steps in their proper sequence for execution of
works according to the Technical Circulars of Water Resources Department.

There were 620 MI schemes covering 127452 ha of created irrigation potential
(IP) up to March 2007 in Wainganga basin. The Department constructed 158
MI schemes with IP of 34902 ha during the period 2007-08 to 2011-12 against
the target of 225 MI schemes for development of 37904 ha of IP after
incurring an expenditure of = 447.73 crore. Thus, 30 per cent of targeted
schemes could not be completed during the period. In total there were 778 MI
schemes covering 162354 ha of created IP in Wainganga Basin up to end of
2011-12.

2.2.3.1 Overlapping of command area of MI schemes due to ill planning

According to para 2.007 and 7.008 of Madhya Pradesh Works Department
(MPWD) Manual, detailed estimates are prepared after completion of survey
and alignment and after approval by EE. SDOs are responsible for preparation
of Detailed Project Report (DPR) after obtaining details on gauge and
discharge, rainfall, meteorological data, agricultural statistics of villages under
submergence and command etc. Instances of command area having
overlapping MI schemes due to improper survey and investigations are
discussed below;

> In Katni division, the work of construction of Bhainswahi MI scheme
envisaged to irrigate 105 ha was taken up in January 2009 ata cost of
88.38 lakh. The scope of work included construction of earthen bund,
excavation and filling of puddle, filter blanket, rock toe, seepage drain,
chipping and pitching, turfing, head sluice, excavation for approach
channel and spill channel, waste weit and fall. The contractor executed
earthwork for earthen bund in entire length excluding length for nalla
closure and sluice (November 2010). Thereafter work was stopped as
the command area of scheme was overlapping with the command area
of right bank canal of Bargi dam. An expenditure of € 36.26 lakh was
incurred till February 2012 on the work.
On this being pointed out in audit E-in-C stated (October 2012) that
even after detailed survey of the scheme and award of headwork in
January 2009, overlapping of command area of Bargi canal was not
known which was noticed only during preparation of land acquisition
cases for canal. It was further stated that Minor schemes often came in
the command area of major schemes and structures constructed for
minor schemes would be utilised for other works.
The reply is not acceptable as work on Bargi Diversion Project was
ongoing since 2001 and overlapping of command area could have been
identified in alignment survey for canal. Thus, due to lack of
coordination between the two Departments of the Government, the
expenditure of हैं 36.26 lakh on survey and construction proved
wasteful.

Vv

In Seoni division, the work of construction of Bothiya MI scheme
envisaged to irrigate 252.94 ha of area was taken up (November 2008).
Till March 2012, 80 per cent of the head work was completed and no
progress in canal work was reported. Further, proposal to acquire the
land for canal system was sent to Land Acquisition Officer (LAO) in
August 2010. An expenditure of 2.58 crore has been incurred up to
2011-12.

We noticed (April 2012) that a part of command area of Bothiya MI

which was a major project undertaken by WRD in 1987-88. The
Department, however, did not take initiative to reduce/modify the
command atea so as to reduce cost of project or cover new command
area. Further, the agreement for work was rescinded in 2011-12 due to
slow progress of work by the contractor. Thus, the expenditure of
% 2.58 crore remained unfruitful so far (October 2012).

E-in-C stated (October 2012) that the scheme was situated at tail of
Pench Diversion Project and partial command of the scheme was
affected by the command of Pench Diversion Project; therefore it was
decided to complete the scheme.

The reply confirms that the division was aware that part of the
command area was overlapping but did not take any corrective action.

The Principal Secretary, WRD stated (September 2012) in exit
conference that the works of major schemes generally covered
command. area of MI Schemes and asserted that MI schemes would
still be useful even alier completion of major projects and supplement
irrigation for additional crop. It was further stated that completion
period of major projects were very long due to land acquisition,
rehabilitation and environmental clearances.

The reply is not acceptable because overlapping of command area
could have been identified at time of alignment survey of canal and
corrective actions for avoiding overlapping of command should have
been taken by the Department as it resulted in avoidable expenditure.

2.2.3.2 Poor planning of designs, survey etc. causing unfruitful expenditure

Para 7.002 and 7.003 of the MPWD Manual provides that the reconnaissance
report™® in respect of medium and minor projects shall be initiated by the EE
and the SDO. After receipt of Administrative Approval (AA) for survey and
investigation, technical sanction for survey estimate shall be accorded by the
officers competent to sanction it and thereafter, detailed survey and
investigation will start for the project. Technical circular (No.53) issued in
February 2006 stipulated that no project was to be submitted above the level of
EE without ensuring general feasibility of project which included ascertaining
sufficiently impervious dam site.

We noticed (December 2011 to May 2012) in three divisions that five MI
schemes covering 607.46 ha of area to be irrigated, were non functional due to
poor planning of designs, survey etc. rendering an expenditure of % 5.89 crore
unfruitful to a large extent as detailed in Appendix 2.15. The failure in
planning for the schemes mainly resulted in;

> non-retaining of water in the tank basin,

* Report for assessment of feasibility of scheme by preliminary survey and investigation

The Principal Secretary, WRD stated (September 2012) in exit conference that
geotechnical investigations were done for only medium and major irrigation
projects.

The reply is not acceptable as the geotechnical investigations were necessary
in terms of the Technical circular. Further, fixing the location of dam/spill
channel foundation in case of minor project is required to be done in
consultation with Engineering Geologist according to Appendix 7.02 of
MPWD Manual. Thus, poor planning resulted in the investment of रैं 5.89
crore remaining largely unfruitful.

2.2.3.3 Plan for acquisition of land

As per note 3 below paragraph 2.104 of MPWD Manual, notification for the
acquisition of land required for any particular work must be submitted before
the work is put in hand. Thus availability of land is the first pre requisite for
proceeding with the construction of any irrigation project.

We noticed (December 2011 to April 2012) in six test checked divisions that
13 MI schemes covering 3057.41 ha of targeted irrigation remained
incomplete due to non-acquisition of land rendering expenditure of हैं 16.89
crore unfruitful as detailed in the Appendix 2.16. The delays in execution
were mainly due to non preparation of plans and time schedule for acquisition
of land.

The Principal Secretary, WRD accepted (September 2012) in exit conference
that there were delays in acquisition of land and stated that to a large extent the
problem has been resolved by continuous monitoring and dialogue with the
Collectors. He further stated that the Department was contemplating to make
payment for land acquisition after notification under Section 9 of the Land
Acquisition Act and revenue authorities were being requested for mutation of
acquired land in favour of WRD within 15 days of payment of compensation,
to avoid any hindrance later on.

The fact however remains that due to taking up works before ensuring
acquisition of land required for the schemes rendered the investment of
₹ 16.89 crore unfruitful.

2.2.5 Execution

2.2.5.1 Excess expenditure over Administrative Approval

As per para 2.005 of MPWD Manual, revised AA of the competent authority
should be obtained when the expenditure exceeds or is likely to exceed the
amount approved by more than 10 per cent. Further, as per note 3(b) below
para 2.120, the amount of the detailed estimate must not exceed the amount
included in the expenditure sanction.

We noticed (February 2012 to April 2012) from records of five divisions”
test checked that excess expenditure on 26 MI schemes was incurred over the
AA to the tune of € 29.34 crore, as हैं 76.67 crore was actually incurred against
the amount of AA of % 47.33 crore. The excess expenditure over AA ranged
between 11.36 per cent and 267.32 per cent as detailed in Appendix 2.17.
However, revised AA was not obtained though the expenditure exceeded the
AA.

The Principal Secretary, WRD stated (September 2012) during exit conference
that excess expenditure over AA was due to price escalation and increased
cost of land acquisition and the process of issuing revised AA was underway.
He further stated that system had been refined and administrative approval for
works was being given after examining feasibility of scheme, detailed survey,
technical sanction and preparation of case for land acquisition.

The fact remains that revised AA was not obtained before incurring excess
expenditure beyond 10 per centover the AA.

> The terms of agreement provided for payment at the estimated rate plus or
