estimates.

2.2.5.2 Non-deposit of performance security by the contractors

As per conditions of contract in Appendix 2.14 (clause 1) of MPWD Manual, the
person whose tender is accepted, shall deposit security alongwith earnest money
in cash or Government securities and amount of shortfall in security will be
recovered from his running bills to make up total amount of security not
exceeding 10 per cent of estimated cost of work.

Further, according to the order of the Government (May 2003), the person whose
tender is accepted shall within 30 days from the date of acceptance letter of his
bid or before entering into an agreement, furnish initial security deposit
(performance security deposit) equal to five per cent of the contract value which
shall include the amount of earnest money deposit. In addition to the above initial
security deposit, deduction shall be made from the running bill equal to 5 per cent
of the total value of each bill. Besides, in terms of rule 21 of MPFC Vol-II, clauses
of the agreement should not be changed without concurrence of the Finance
Department.

We noticed (December 2011 to April 2012) that the decision for excluding the
clause of five per cent security deposit was taken by committee headed by E-in-C
in June 2006 on submission of application in this respect by the contractors. The
Department did not obtain the performance security deposit amounting 0 हैं 3.72
crore in 50 agreements of six divisions as detailed in Appendix 2.18. We also
observed that the clause of five per cent security deposit was reintroduced from
March 2011. The action of deleting the clause of five per cent performance
security at E-in-C level without consent of the Finance Department was irregular
and led to undue financial aid of % 3.72 crore to the contractors. Besides,
performance of the works was not secured as discussed in paragraph 2.2.5.6.

The Principal Secretary, WRD stated (September 2012) during exit conference
that contractors quoted rates considering terms and conditions of the contracts
and deduction of performance security would have impacted on their quoted

The reply is not acceptable as the department did not have powers to make any
changes in the contract document without obtaining the consent of the Finance
Depattment in terms of provisions of MPFC and the department did not
provide any evidence that by making changes to the contract conditions, the
department derived cost effectiveness in execution of the work.

2.2.5.3 Non-deduction of additional security deposit for unbalanced items

According to standard clause 3.28 of the agreements, in case the approving
authority considers that the tenderer has quoted disproportionately high rates
for some items when compared to the estimated rate of such items, the tender
is to be considered unbalanced and he shall have the power to limit the
payment of such items to the estimated rate of that item plus or minus overall
tender percentage above/below as the case may be. The withheld amount on
such items shall be retained by the Engineer-in-charge as additional security
deposit. Lf the contractor leaves the work incomplete for any reason, the
additional security deposit so deducted shall stand forfeited to the
Government.

We noticed (December 2011 to April 2012) in five divisions® that divisional
officers did not recover the amount of additional security deposit on account
of unbalanced rates of items of works to the tune of % 3.87 crore in 13 on-
going works as detailed in the Appendix 2.19, Non-retention of amount of
additional security deposit for unbalanced items of works was contrary to the
provisions of the agreement.

The Principal Secretary, WRD stated (September 2012) during exit conference
that the Department had taken corrective measures in this regard and tenders
were being invited on percentage rate basis.

by considering all the expenses for its construction as well as for maintenance.
According to the directions of WRD, electric connection for LIS was to be
taken by beneficiary farmers. There were 44 completed LISs in the Basin of
which 18 LISs were examined in six divisions. Instances of partial execution

2.2.5.6 Execution of earthwork below specification

Para 2.028 of MPWD Manual provides that an officer according technical
sanction to an estimate is responsible for soundness of design and for
incorporating all the items required for inclusion in the estimate with reference
to the drawings. Para 4.9.9 (section-II, volume-I) of specifications for
irtigation projects (of the Unified Schedule of Rates for works) provides that
in the earth fill embankment watered, rolled and compacted at optimum
moisture content and at dry density, settlement allowance of one per cent and
two per cent of designed height for unyielding (rock) and compressible (soil)
foundations, respectively shall be deducted from the executed quantity.
According to irrigation specifications, earthwork on bund includes the laying
of earth in 15 centimetre layer, watering and compaction of earthwork at
optimum moisture content to achieve maximum dry density.

We noticed (April 2012 and May 2012) that:

> In Katni division, the G schedule of the work of Jamunchua MI scheme
included an item of watering for earthwork. Payment for 1,17,874.38
cu m of watering was made (March 2010) but in the next running bills,
the up to date quantity of watering was shown as nil indicating that no
watering was done. Shrinkage allowance deducted at 20 per cent, also
confirmed that watering was not done. Thus, the execution of
earthwork valued at हैं 56.66 lakh “was below specification which may
result in rapid depletion of water due to seepage.

The E-in-C stated (October 2012) that disciplinary action would be
taken against the field staff for non-execution of work as per
established norms.

> Technical circular 27 issued (August 1990) by E-in-C provides that
alignment of the puddle trench” should be fixed in such a way that the
downstream edge of its bottom width should coincide with the central
line of the dam crust throughout its length. Moreover, at sites where
the cost of complete seepage cut off is not big, it should always be
provided even if the loss of water due to under seepage would have no
importance. Thus, puddle trench is required to be executed in the entire
length of earthen dam

In Hiran division, for the balance work of Imlai MI scheme, no work
was indicated in measurement book from RD metre 126.70 to RD

(March 2013)

2.2.5.7 Excess payments to the contractors

As per the orders of GOMP (October 1987) forming part of agreement, EE
shall have no powers of determining the rates for extra items and prior
sanction of CE shall be necessary for execution of and payments for additional
quantities of more than 10 per cent in case of works for which technical
sanction has been accorded by CE. Instances of excess payments to the
contractors noted in audit are discussed below;

n

> In construction of Patharia MI scheme by Seoni division, we observed.

that the estimated quantity of earthwork of 53691.36 cum was included
in the G schedule. The contractor executed 71179.45 cum of earthwork
and was paid at the rate of हैं 50 per cum, Thus, the quantities of the
work were exceeded and proposal for approval of rates for payment of
quantities in excess of 10 per cent was sent (June 2011) to the CE. The
CE granted permission for payment of excess executed quantity at 90
per cent of the worked out rate of the items under clause 4.3.13.3 (a) of
the agreement or the quoted rate of the contractor, whichever was
lower. Therefore, the payment for excess quantities of more than 10
per cent was to be regulated at the rate of € 36.42 per cum”! according
to orders of the CE. The division however paid the contractor at the
rate of € 50 per cu m. Payment at higher rate led to excess payment of
% 1.64 lakh” to the contractor. We further observed that an amount of
% 7.52 lakh was paid to the contractor for execution of extra items

2.2.5.10 Irregularities in works through MNREGS funds

As per directions issued (order no 4823, dated 20 December 2007) by the
Panchayat and Rural Development Department, the Water User Associations
shall be the executing agency for the maintenance work of minor irtigation
tanks and canals undertaken through MNREGS funds.

We noticed (February 2012 to April 2012) that:

> In Dindori division, the works amounting to हैं 12.96 crore for
maintenance of minor schemes were executed departmentally by the
division using the MNREGS funds. There were 14 WUAs constituted
for 44 minor irrigation schemes in the year 2010-11 and 23 WUAs
were constituted for 64 minor irrigation schemes in the year 2011-12
covering 8627.270 ha and 12574.27 ha respectively. Execution of
works of MNREGS directly by the sub-divisional authorities was thus
irregular. This deprived the WUAs {rom participating in the works
under MNREGS which was against the requirement of the said
provision of Panchayat and Rural Development Department.

The E-in-C inter alia stated (October 2012) that WUAs did not
propose any work under MNREGS and the EE was fixed as executing
agency for MNREGS works.

The reply is not acceptable as the Department could not get WUAs
associated in execution of works as required in the directives of the
Panchayat and Rural Development Department.

The form of muster roll prescribed in directions issued (January 2006)
by Panchayat and Rural Development Department provides for
signature of witnesses at the time of payment to labourers and name of
banks where accounts of labourers are maintained. Further, according
to para 4.013 of MPWD Manual, muster roll is not used within three
months of issue, it should be reissued. Cutting and use of correction
fluid in the muster roll is restricted.

In four divisions, an amount of € 49.43 lakh “was paid to the labourers
through nominal muster rolls (NMRs) but NMRs were used after three
months, signature of witnesses were not found recorded and the name
of the bank was not recorded against the payment made to the

The fact remains that non-compliance of provisions of MPWD manual in
undertaking remodeling work resulted in deteriorating conditions of canals,
which ultimately resulted in short utilisation of irrigation potential.

2.2.6.2 Inadequate inspection of works at division level
Para 8.016 of WD Manual prescribes that annual/periodical inspection of:

(D All head works of major/ medium /minor projects with culturable
command area above 500 hectares annually and head works of other
minor projects once in four years and

(ii) Canals with carrying capacity of more than three cumecs annually and
canals carrying less than three cumecs at least once in four years should be
carried out by the Executive Engineer.

We noticed (March 2012 and April 2012) in four divisions out of the six test
checked divisions that during 2007-12, inspections by the EE were not done
according to the required frequency as detailed in the table below:

Table 2.21

Para 15.2.2 of IS 456: 2000, prescribes minimum frequency of sampling of

Further, standard clause 14.0 of agreements for Madhya Pradesh Water Sector
Restructuring Programme (MPWSRP) provides that contractor shall arrange to
take sample of cement concrete as directed by Engineer-in-Charge at random
to evaluate the quality of concrete lining laid in respective reaches of canal an
frequency of taking sample core shall be determined by the Engineer which
should be one sample from bed lining and another sample from side lining for
every 250 M length for core test.

We noticed (March 2012 and April 2012) in Katni and Narsinghpur divisions
out of the six divisions test checked, that in three works, only 51 samples were
taken against the required 105 samples for testing the compressive strength. In
six works of the Katni division, no test was carried out. The details are given
in Appendix 2.20.

The EE of Katni division stated (April 2012) that work was done according to
specification and field tests carried out such as, slump etc. He further stated
that in the absence of test results, appropriate amount had been deducted from
contractors bills.

The reply does not explain the reason for not conducting test for compressive
strength and the fact remains that deduction of amount for not conducting the
tests for prescribed samples indicated that the works were accepted without
proper quality control.

Similarly, in respect of the work of restructuring of head work, main canal and
RBC of Dhorsi regulator under MPWSRP (Katni division), the prescribed core
tests were not done for the work of lining. Thus, divisional authorities
accepted the work without assuring the quality of the work

The EE stated (April 2012) that the test results were awaited from the
contractors. Further, E-in-C stated (October 2012) that new structure for
quality control is being proposed. It was also stated that work of quality
control was affected due to lack of trained officials and lack of equipment,
though it does not mean that quality of works have been affected.

The reply is not acceptable as the quality control over the work could not be
assured due to lesser tests than the required frequency of tests or non-testing.

62


Chapter It Performance Audit

2.2.7 Conclusion

The construction of Minor Irrigation schemes in Wainganga Basin of Water
Resources Department was affected due to inadequate survey, non-
synchronisation of process of land acquisition with tendering process and
award of work in mid-way of acquisition of land. Performance of Lift
Irrigation Schemes was poor due to lack of participation of users. Overlapping
of command area of Minor Irrigation schemes with other Irrigation Schemes
was not identified within time resulting in wasteful expenditure. Monitoring
and quality control mechanism were deficient as basic records were not
maintained and inadequate samples were being taken for testing

Narmada Valley Development Department

[2 EIT | Thematic paragraph on Distribution Network of Rani Avanti Bai
Lodhi Sagar Project

[2.3.1 Introd Introduction

Rani Avanti Bai Lodhi Sagar (RABLS) Project previously known as Bargi
Multipurpose Project, located in Jabalpur district was the first project on
Narmada River under Narmada Valley Development Programme. The Project
consisting of dam and distribution network was initiated with the objective to
irrigate 1.57 lakh hectare (ha) through Left Bank Canal (LBC). Right Bank
Canal (RBC) is separate project and it was not examined in this review.

The distribution network consisting of LBC including main canal, branch
canal, distributaries, minors, sub-minors, water course and field channels**
under RABLS Project is a major irtigation project to provide irrigation
facilities in Jabalpur (95000 ha) and Narsinghpur (62000 ha) district. The cost
of LBC was initially met out by the State funds but due to paucity of fund it
was taken up under National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development
(NABARD) loan since 1996-97. Construction of 135.50 km long left bank
main canal along with its 1915.04 km distribution network was taken up by
the Irrigation Department (renamed as Water Resources Department in July
1985) in 1982 at a cost of हैं 251.85 crore and was to be completed in 1990.
The RABLS project was still incomplete after spending = 1099.66 crore as of
March 2012. Irrigation potential of 1.23 lakh hectare was created till January
2012 against the target of 1.57 lakh hectare as compiled by us. However,
balance works for creation of IP have not been started till March 2012.

The Nannada Valley Development Authority (NVDA, hereinafter referred to
as the Authority) was constituted under Narmada Valley Development
Department (NVDD) in 1985 with a view to give special attention for
development of irrigation facilities in the Narmada Basin. The Authority took
over the construction of distribution network in 1992 from Water Resources
Department (WRD). The Minister of Narmada Valley Development
Department in Madhya Pradesh is the ex-officio chairman of the Authority,
who is assisted by a Vice Chairman and five {ull time members (Engineering,
Finance, Power, Environment and Forest & Rehabilitation). The distribution
network of the RABLS project is being executed under the Chief Engineer,
RABLS Project, Jabalpur. The Chief Engineer (CE) is assisted by three
Superintending Engineers (SEs) and four Executive Engineers (EEs) at the
field level.

The audit of distribution network of RABLS Project was undertaken with a
view to assess whether the funds were available and utilised effectively,

received till March 2012. Another revised estimate for = 1552.85 crore was
submitted in March 2012, which has also not been approved by the
Government so far (March 2013), The Authority incurred total expenditure of
₹ 1099.66 crore on the distribution network up to 2011-12. Thus, revised AA
was not obtained prior to incurring excess expenditure over the AA as
required under Para 2.005(1) of Madhya Pradesh Works Department
(MPWD) manual which stipulates that when the expenditure exceeds 20 per
cent of the sanctioned estimate, revised AA should be obtained

2.3.3.1 Non-availing of NABARD loan

According to the schedule II, special terms and conditions of NABARD,
Government of Madhya Pradesh (GOMP) shall ensure timely execution of
projects by meticulous planning and adequate monitoring in view of the
limited period available for construction of canal for irrigation.

Loans aggregating = 576.96 crore were sanctioned under Rural Infrastructure
Development Fund (RIDF)*” by NABARD in six phases between 1996-97
and 2005-06. According to the schedule of disbursement of the loan, the
whole of the sanctioned amount was to be disbursed by the end of 2006-07 by
way of reimbursing expenditure already incurred by the project Authorities.

The reasons for shortfall in utilisation of the loan were delays in completion

and non-synchronisation of the works of canal network with the main canal
and contractors leaving the work midway.

utilisation of fund was the contractors leaving the work incomplete. The CE,
however, did not explain the other factors contributing to delay in completion
of work as stated above.

2.3.4 Creation of Irrigation Potential (IP) targets and achievement

Specific targets and milestones were prescribed in the Detailed Project Report
(DPR) for execution and creation of the specified irrigation potential to avoid
delay and ensure timely accrual of the intended benefits. The works were to
be executed according to the provisions of the Madhya Pradesh Works
Department Manual.

(ii) Delay in award of work

Clause 4.7 of Appendix 2.10 of MPWD Manual stipulates that tender should
be accepted within four months from the date of tender. Clause 8.1.1 of
Appendix 2.10 of MPWD Manual as well as agreement (clause 2.10) further
stipulates that the contractor will execute the agreement within 30 days of the
date of acceptance of his tender.

We noticed in respect of 12 works (design IP 52,111 hectare) out of 21 works
test checked in four divisions® that there were delays in issue of work order
(three to 11 months) in four cases”? and delays at EE level in sending Notice
Inviting Tender (NIT) programme to higher authority (eight to 36 months) in
other four cases”!, There were delays of five to 12 months in five cases” for
finalising NIT and delays of four to 13 months in ten cases™ in acceptance of
tenders at the Authority level. In two cases”, tenders for balance work were
not finalised even after a delay of 24 to 36 months from the date of rescission
of original work. Thus, in respect of these works, there were delays at
different levels and stages as detailed in Appendix 2.21.

In particular, four works (Sl. No. five to eight of Appendix 2.21) of
construction of LBC from km 130.67 to km 135.50 with its distribution
system (design IP 34,223 hectare) were taken up in 2005-06 to be completed
between July 2008 to December 2009 which was still incomplete (March
2012). All the four works were rescinded between July 2009 and October
2010 due to slow progress of works by the contractors. Tenders for three
balance works were accepted with the delays of seven to 13 months and in
one case the decision of rejection and invitation of tender with splitting the
work in four parts, was taken after delay of nine months.

Regarding delay in finalising of tender, the EE Canal division no. 1 Kareli
replied that it was only a procedural delay which was not attributable to the
department. The reply is not correct because tender for all balance works (SI.
No. five to 12 of Appendix 2.21) were not finalised even after delays of 19 to
39 months from the date of rescission. In two balance works (Sl. No. 11 and
12 of Appendix 2.21), tenders were not invited even after a lapse of 24 to 36
months.

(iii) Contractors leaving the work incomplete

Eight works for construction of canals (for creation of [P of 50,321 ha) out of
21 works test checked by us, were awarded to various contractors from April
2003 to March 2008 for completion by October 2004 to December 2009
(within 13 to 21 months). All the eight works were rescinded between June
2007 and October 2010 due to poor progress by the contractors. Out of this,
one work (Nagna distributary) was completed (June 2011) through another
contractor with a delay of seven years and extra cost of = 2.93 crore which is
recoverable from original contractor. Balance seven works were still
incomplete (July 2012).

As the Authority did not have adequate security deposit in their contracts,
which could have been adjusted if the contractor failed to complete the work,
it was unable to ensure completion of works by the contractors, which are
discussed in paragraphs 6.1 to 6.4. Thus, the works for creation of 50,321
hectare IP were delayed from two to seven years as the contractors left the
works incomplete as detailed in Appendix 2.22.

(iv) Non-completion of work in scheduled period

Time schedule is the essence of any agreement. It was, however, observed
that none of the works awarded to contractors for construction of distribution
network was completed within the scheduled time. Scrutiny of all the 10
completed works during May 2006 to March 2012 indicated that there were
delays ranging up to six years in completion of works due to delay in
acquisition of land, shifting of electric lines, handing over drawing & design
and execution of excess quantities. Delays in acquisition of land in all 10
cases were significant as detailed in Appendix 2.23.

2.3.4.3 Inadequate planning resulting in non-creation of IP

According to paragraph 2.025 and 2.026 of the MPWD Manual, project
reports are required to be prepared on the basis of detailed survey and
investigation. The designs and estimates should also conform to the relevant
Indian Standards. Para 2.028 of the MPWD Manual further provides that the
officer according the technical sanction to an estimate is responsible for
ensuring the soundness of design

(i) Inadequate drainage system

Hareri branch canal from km 31.30 to km 52.83, constructed between 1992
and 2002 at a cost of € 1.60 crore to irrigate 3,093 ha of Narsinghpur district,
was an unlined canal. After a lapse of five years, the canal was tested for the
first time in 2007. During testing, the canal breached at km 33.18. After
rectification, the canal was tested in the year 2008-09 but failed again and
breached at km 37.50 km. It was also rectified in 2008-09. During running of
water in 2009-10, the canal again breached at km 43.500, 47.700, 48.100,
§2.400. During rains, various other reaches of canal also got damaged.

GS. Institute of Technology & Science, Indore authorised by the department
for analysing the reasons for damage of canal, reported that the canal was

damaged due to the absence of properly designed cross drainage and use of
unsuitable soil in the embankment. The technical advisor of the Authority also
reported (December 2009) in his inspection report that the canal was not
constructed according to earth dam specifications although it was passing
through the local ridges and the valleys of the ravines near the river Narmada.

We further noticed that Hareri Branch canal from km 31.30 to km 52.83 was
completed during 1992 to 2002 but it was not run immediately and was lying
idle due to non-completion of previous reaches (km 00 to km 31.30). As a
result, maintenance work is also not being undertaken between km, 31.30 and
km, 52.83. Besides, the canal was aligned along the ridge, only one Cross
Drainage” (CD) was provided at km 33.19 in the entire reach. In the estimate
(December 2011) for reconstruction of breached canal, two additional CDs in
km 38.50 & km 41.50 were provided which indicates that the previously
planned design for drainage was deficient. Tenders of % 3.20 crore, to correct
the damaged work was yet to be invited (March 2012). Therefore, the
expenditure of % 1.60 crore on construction of the canal became substantially
unfruitful and 3,093 ha IP could not be utilised so far.

The Vice Chairman of the Authority agreed with the audit contention and
stated in the exit conference (June 2012) that breach of the canal was a
technical failure and the reconstruction work would be done as per Central
Water Commissions recommendation.

(ii) Non-planning for outlets and re-sectioning work

Earth work, lining, structures and outlets between km 66 and km 84 of main
canal, and its branches and distributaries was awarded by the EE Canal Dn
no. 2 Gotegaon in March 2002 for हैं 17.26 crore to be completed by
September 2004. After execution of work valued at = 15.68 crore by the
contractor, the Authority withdrew the balance work (% 1.58 crore) in
September 2008 stating that:

(i) The outlets could not be constructed due to the reason that the exact place
of the outlet shall be decided during execution of the field channels.

(ii) Cement Concrete (CC) lining could not be completed as the earth work of
the minor canal, which was completed long back, had settled down and
required re-sectioning work which was not in the scope of agreement.

The reasons on which the work was withdrawn indicate that the work was
taken up without properly determining place of outlets and items of work that
would be required to be executed. Inadequate planning for the work, thus
resulted in avoidable extra cost of % 5.17 crore”® for the balance work and
non-creation of IP of 1,793 ha. We further noticed that there was no provision
for re-sectioning of the canal system even in the revised estimate.

The alignment of the canals crosses the natural drainage valleys and provision has to
be made to take the drains safely across the canal without damaging it

2.3.5 Poor utilisation of created Irrigation Potential

The Authority had created IP of 1.15 lakh ha up to March 2008 which was
increased to 1.23 lakh ha up to January 2012 but against this utilisation ranged
from 7,903 ha to 30,923 ha. Thus, the utilisation was only eight to 25 per cent
during 2007-08 to 2011-12 of the created IP as detailed below:

The reasons for poor utilisation of created IP were non-construction of Water
Courses”’ (WC) and Field Channels”* (FC) and non-running of completed
canal in full swing due to various reasons such as silting, non-construction of
structures, damages of structures and breach of canal in various places as
discussed in the succeeding paragraphs.

2.3.5.1 Non-construction of Water Course/Field Channel simultaneously
with canal network

The canal system comprises of Main Canal, Branch Canal, Distributaries,
Minors, Sub-Minors, WC and FC. Approved DPR (1989) of LBC network
provided for construction of WC/FC in 1.57 lakh ha for maximum utilisation
of created IP.

We noticed that WC/FC essential to carry water from the canal to the fields
were not constructed simultaneously with the canal network. The work of
WC/FC for 17,870 ha out of 1.23 lakh ha created IP was completed through
Command Area Development Authority”? (CADA) between 1991 and 2004.
Thereafter, the work of WC/FC was stopped and no effort was made by the
Authority to complete the balance work. In 2009-10, the work of WC/FC was
again taken up under National Rural Employment Guarantee Scheme
(NREGS) and CADA. The works of WC/FC were completed only for 10,194

” Water course: A channel taking off from a canal which conveys water to the turn out

Field channel: A channel taking off from the Government outlet and leading to the
farm gate

” GOT launched a Centrally Sponsored Scheme of Command Area Development
Programme in 1974-75 with the objective to bridge the gap between the irrigation
potential created and that utilised through developing the command area. This
programme is monitored by constituting a multi-disciplinary team under the overall
control of the Command Area Development Authorities

98

72


Chapter Il Performance Audit

ha between 2009-10 and 2011-12, WC/FC works were ongoing for 4,830 ha
and for remaining 89,705 ha, works were not started (as of March 2012).
Irrigation was being done by cultivators by lifting the water from the canal or
by using their own arrangements. Thus, the benefit of irrigation was available
only to cultivators having fields near the canal. Non-construction of WC/FC
simultaneously with canal system deprived the cultivators from the benefit of
irrigation even after completion of the canal

In the exit conference, the Vice Chairman of the Authority stated (June 2012)
that non-construction of WC/FC was not the only reason for less utilisation of
IP but non accounting of utilisation of irrigation potential, irregular rain and
unwillingness of farmers to change crop pattern (requiring more water) also
caused lesser utilisation of IP. He further added that delay in completion of
the work of WC/FC was mainly due to frequent changes in the policy of the
Central Government regarding development of command area for such
project and the work of WC/FC would be completed early.

The fact remains that due to delay in completion of the work of WC/FC, the
cultivators who were not in proximity to the canal were deprived of the
facility of irrigation and resulting in less utilisation of IP of 92,878 hectare

2.3.5.2 Non-running of completed canal

For maximum utilisation of created IP (1.23 lakh ha), it was necessary to run
the canal in full capacity up to the tail.

We noticed that 17 per cent of the created IP was not utilised due to non-
running of completed canal in full capacity. Out of 590 minors/sub-minors,
143 minors/sub-minors having IP of 20,437 ha were not in a condition to
carry water due to silting in bed (58 minors/sub-minors; IP 9,852 ha), non-
construction of structures like head regulator, village road bridge and cross
drainage (55 minors/sub-minors; IP 6,636 ha), breach of canal (24
minors/sub-minors; IP 3,093 ha) and damage in canal structures (six
minors/sub-minors; IP 856 ha). The main reason for silting in canal bed of
minors/ sub-minors was its non-maintenance. Action for rectifying damage
and construction of incomplete structures was also not initiated on time,
which caused lesser utilisation of Irrigation Potential.

The matter was reported to Government (August 2012) and reply has not been
received so far (March 2013).

Instances of violation of the provisions of the agreements and the Manual
leading to inadmissible payment, excess payment and irregular release of
Security Deposit (SD) /Performance Security Deposits (PSD) were noticed
which are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs.

Irregular release of Security Deposit/Performance Security Deposit

In terms of the standard clause 4.3.17.2 of the agreement, SD/PSD of the
contractor shall be released only after twelve months from the date of
completion of the work. Clause 4.3.3.3 of the agreement stipulates that if the
contractor fails to complete the works, competent authority shall have power
to measure up the work of the contractor and entrust the unexecuted part of
the work to another contractor. Any extra cost in the execution of the balance
work shall be borne and paid by the original contractor which may be
adjusted from SD/PSD of the contractor and any money due to him by the
Government.

We noticed that both the above agreements were rescinded on 22.12.08 under
clause 4.3.3.3 of the agreement due to slow progress of work. Therefore, the
balance work was to be completed through another agency and the extra cost
should have been adjusted from the contractors deposits. Contrary to this, the
EE Canal division no-2, Jabalpur irregularly released two Fixed Deposit
Receipts (FDRs) deposited by the contractor towards PSD for हैं 1.54 लण
to the contractors on 22 November 2009, that is, after recession of the
agreements as detailed in the table below:

The CE accepted (March 2012) the audit observation and replied that release

2.3.6.2 Inflated measurement of works resulting in excess payment

The MPWD Manual envisaged that running payments to the contractor should
be made after recording measurements by the Sub Divisional Officer. 10 per
cent of such measurements should be test checked by the Divisional O

Two works'®’ were awarded (August 2004 and November 2006) by the EE
Canal Dn, no. 1 Kareli to contractors for ¥ 32.87 crore with the scheduled
completion period by May 2006 and July 2008 respectively. Due to slow
progress, the works were rescinded in March 2010 and July 2009,
respectively,

We noticed in the examination of final bills of the said incomplete works that
the executed quantities of several items of work in final bills were less than
the quantities for which payments were made up to the penultimate bills. The
reason was non-deduction of excavated soil used in embankment from earth
work, non-deduction of shrinkage allowances, excavated quantities of
Cohesive Non-swelling Soil (CNS)! material not deducted from execution
of CNS and wrong calculation in previous bills.

The excess payment of % 62.20 lakh (Appendix 2.24) due to inflated
measurement up to the penultimate bills signifies that the EE failed to
exercise control over the measurements recorded in the measurement books.

The EE Disnet Division, Narsinghpur accepted (January 2012) the excess
payment due to inflated measurement and stated that it would be adjusted
from the contractors deposits. The payments on inflated measurement also
resulted in cost overrun of the project without creating tangible assets for the
project.

2.3.6.3 Avoidable payment of escalation

According to the Appendix 1.25 (vi) of the MPWD Manual it is the
responsibility of the Executive Engineer to work out the requirement of land
for work, quarries and draw up programme for land acquisition/land transfer
with a view to ensure transfer of required land before target date set for
starting of works.

In three agreements (Appendix 2.25) of canal works intended to provide
irrigation in 25,675 ha, we noticed that works were not completed within the
stipulated period due to delay in acquisition of land. The competent authority
granted time extension to the contractors on this ground. According to the
clause 2.40.2 of the agreements, the contractors were entitled for escalation in
me (i) Construction from km 126.190 to km 130.670 of LBC and its distribution system
(ii) Construction of Sadumar Branch Canal from km 0.0 to km 4.5 & Dungaria
Distributary km 5 to km 23

They are soils possessing the property of cohesion of varying degree and having

non-expanding type clay minerals such as illite and kaolinite and their combination

with low plasticity with liquid limit not exceeding 50 per cent,

76


Chapter It Performance Audit

the extended period for delay not attributable to them. We further observed
that works were awarded cither without initiating the process of land
acquisition or land were acquired after 27 to 39 months from the date of
award of work to the contractors. This had resulted in increase in schedule
completion period and avoidable payment of escalation for the period beyond
the stipulated period to the contractors of ¥ 4.81 crore. Thus due to delay in
completion of work from one year to three and a half years as detailed in
Appendix 2.25, there was consequential delay in providing irrigation facility
in 25,675 ha.

The EE Disnet Dn, Narsinghpur (January 2012) stated that the proposal of
land acquisition for branch canal was submitted before start of the work,
whereas the proposal of minors and sub-minors was submitted later due to
paucity of funds as the work was sanctioned under NABARD in February
2003. The reply however does not give explanation for award of works
without acquisition of land. Other two divisions did not give any reply. The
excess payment of escalation would also increase the cost of the project.

The matter was also reported to the Government (August 2012) and reply has
not been received so far (March 2013).

2.3.6.4 Non-observance of terms and conditions of contracts resulting in
undue financial benefits to contractors

We noticed that the Authority, on many occasions, failed to enforce the
provisions of the contracts to secure its interest or avoid loss to the
Government, which also resulted in undue financial benefits to the contractors
as summarised below:

Nature of Audit observation

Irregularities

Audit observation

contracts were rescinded under clause 4.3.3.3 of the
agreement instead of clause 4.3.2. The rescission of
contract under 4.3.2 was more advantageous to the
Government because the additional expenditure incurred
for the completion of balance work was also recoverable
from the original contractor, in addition to forfeiture of the
security deposit and black listing of contractors for award
of further works. Thus, by not invoking clause 4.3.2, the
Authority lost the opportunity to recover extra cost
incurred on completion of balance work from the original
contractor, resulting in overall increase in the project cost
and avoidable loss to the Government of हैं 37.06 crore as
detailed in Appendix 2.26.

We further noticed that though the contracts were
terminated under clause 4.3.3.3 of the agreement, the
division forfeited SD and PSD of ® 1.82 crore only out of
% 7.02 crore of the contractors, which was available with
the Authority.

During the exit conference, the Vice Chairman of the
Authority accepted and stated (June 2012) that legal advice
would be taken to revise the order of rescission of the
contracts issued by the EE, so that the extra cost could be
recovered from defaulting contractors. The fact remains
that rescinding the works in non-panel clause resulted in
cost overrun of the project.

(ii) Excess
payment to
contractors
due to non-

deposit for
unbalanced
items

Clause 3.26 of the agreement provided that if the rates
quoted by the contractor for different items are
disproportionately high as compared to the estimated rates
of such items, payment for those items is to be made at the
estimated rate plus/minus overall tender percentage. The
balance payments are to be withheld as additional security
deposit until completion of the entire work. The withheld
amount will, however, be forfeited if the contractor
abandons the work. Besides the provision in the
agreement, the tender approving authority also specifically
directed (in their acceptance letters) to regulate payment of
unbalanced items as per clause 3.26 of the agreement.

In scrutiny of 12 agreements of LBC and its distribution
network, we noticed that the contractor had quoted higher
rates than the estimated rates for some items of work, for
which an amount of % 9.51 crore was required to be
retained towards additional security deposit. Out of the 12
agreements, seven agreements were rescinded due to slow

We noticed in construction of Sadumar branch canal from

contractor left the work incomplete (July 2009) and
balance amount of € 21.50 lakh remained unrecovered in
the last bill of the contractor taking into account the
erroneous adjustment of हैं 10.19 lakh in that bill.

The Government replied (December 2012) that only
11.31 lakh of secured advance was outstanding for
recovery in the final bill against which precast tiles
amounting to हैं 18 lakh was available with the division. The
reply was not correct as हैं 10.19 lakh was erroneously
adjusted in the final bill and therefore, हैं 21.50 lakh
(₹ 11.31 lakh plus हैं 10.19 lakh) was outstanding for
recovery. Further, value of lying material on site cannot deem
adjusted against the recoverable amount unless it was
consumed in the works as such adjustments inflate the cost of
the project without corresponding benefits in terms of
irrigation potential.

3.1 Department Centric Audit of Narmada Valley Development
Authority

Highlights

Narmada river, originates from Amarkantak Hill in Shahdol district and flows
westwards over a length of 1,312 km before draining through the Gulf of
Cambay (Khambat) into the Arabian Sea in the Bharuch district of Gujarat
State. As there were disputes over the sharing of water among the riparian
States, the Government of Gujarat submitted (July 1968) under the Inter State
Water Dispute Act, 1956, a formal complaint to the Ministry of Water
Resources for sharing of Narmada water and a tribunal (NWDT) was set up in
1969 to adjudicate in the matter of sharing of water. According to the award of
tribunal in December 1979 Madhya Pradesh was to get 18.25 MAF of water of
Narmada. This was to be reviewed by the NWDT in 2024. For expeditious
development of irrigation facilities in the Narmada basin, the Government of
Madhya Pradesh established (July 1981) Narmada Valley Development
Department (NVDD). In July 1985, planning and execution of major projects
in the Narmada Valley was entrusted to the multi disciplinary authority of the
Narmada Valley Development Department (NVDA). So far, 11 dams for 13
major projects across the Narmada and its tributaries for storage of 9.114
Million Acre Feet water and canal system for utilisation of 3.1565 Million
Acre Feet water could be completed. A department centric audit on
Preparedness of State for tapping of Narmada Water as per Narmada Water
Dispute Tribunal award revealed:

> There were slippages in planning for utilisation of Narmada water due

to slow progress by the contractor, delay in obtaining environment
clearances and delay in preparation of resettlement and rehabilitation

plans.
(Paragraph 3.6)
> There were delays in completion of all the components of projects
execution resulting in cost overrun and non-completion of dams as
well canals and distribution system.
(Paragraph 3.7)
> Delays in approval of drawing, design and Detailed Project Reports
(DPRs) were due to non-submission of reports in prescribed format,
incomplete information, inadequate drawings etc.
(Paragraph 3.7.2)
> Delays in obtaining environment clearances were due to submission of
incomplete information to the Ministry of Environment and Forest
(MOEF), delay in preparation and submission of Environment Impact
Analysis/Environment Management Plan.

(Paragraph 3.7.3)

> The delays in acceptance of tenders and award of work contributed to
delays in completion of projects.

(Paragraph 3.7.6)

85



Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2012

> Execution of canals works were delayed mainly due to slow progress
by contractors, delays in land acquisition in few reaches, inadequate
estimates and execution of works in a phased manner for different
reaches instead of taking up execution of all the reaches
simultaneously.
(Paragraph 3.7.7 (ii))
> The scheduled completion period of 18 to 36 months in respect of eight
turnkey contracts had already lapsed. All the works were however,
incomplete as of August 2012 after delays of 12 to 20 months.
(Paragraph 3.7.7(iii))
> Survey works for medium and minor projects (2.677 Million Acre Feet
for 710000 ha) in remaining portion of the Narmada basin
(Amarkantak to Handia gauge) was not awarded as of August 2012.
(Paragraph 3.8)
> After lapse of more than 33 years from the date of award of the
Tribunal, total utilisation of water was only 5.51 Million Acre Feet
during the water year ending June 2012 against the allotted share.
(Paragraph 3.9)
> After imposition of the ceiling by State Planning Commission,
Narmada Valley Development Authority has not so far planned to tie
up funds for bridging the gap of = 11002 crore required for completion
of remaining projects.
(Paragraph 3.10)

3.1 Introduction

The Narmada river,
a Water in the fifth longest river
in India, covers 20
districts of Madhya
Pradesh (५०). It

MiMadhya Pradesh Gujarat Rajasthan ml Maharashtra Cambay  (Khambat)

into the Arabian Sea
Source: Information provided by NVDA

in the Bharuch district of Gujarat State. As there were disputes over the
sharing of water among the riparian States, the Government of Gujarat
submitted (July 1968) under the Inter State Water Dispute Act, 1956, a
formal complaint for sharing of Narmada water to Ministry of Water Resources,
Government of India.

Narmada Water Disputes Tribunal (NWDT) was constituted in October 1969
to adjudicate upon the dispute regarding sharing of Narmada water. The

86


Chapter IIT Department Centric Audit

Tribunal determined the utilisable quantum of Narmada waters to be 28
million acre feet (MAF) at 75 per cent dependability and allocated it among
the four riparian states through its award (Award) in December 1979 as
detailed in the table below:

Table No: 3.1

(Source: Information provided by NVDA)
The award further provided:

> — That apportionment was to be done on the basis of actual withdrawals
and not consumptive use!.

> — That within its share of water, each party State was free to make such
changes in the pattern of water use and in the areas to be benefited
within or outside the Narmada basin in its territory as it may consider
necessary.

> Review at any time after a period of 45 years from the date of
publication (December 1979) of the decision of the Tribunal in the
Official Gazette, with regard to the equitable allocation of water.

Thus, the State of Madhya Pradesh has to ensure that the allocated share
of 18.25 MAF water is fully utilised by creation of necessary
infrastructure by 2024 to avoid reallocation of unutilised water during
review by the NWDT in or after 2024.

The Narmada Valley Development Department (NVDD) was created in 1981
with the objective of executing major irrigation and multipurpose projects in
the Narmada Valley. Execution of irrigation projects till 1985 was undertaken
by the Government of Madhya Pradesh through the Inrigation Department

The NVDA was constituted in July 1985 for specific purpose of planning the
construction of hydropower and irrigation projects in Narmada Basin and
execution of major irrigation projects for utilisation of apportioned share of
Narmada water before review year of the NWDT award. The organisation is
mandated to meet its manpower requirements by recruitment through
deputation from other Government Departments and re-employment of retired
personnel besides hiring of consultants for a fixed period.

3.2 Organisational set-up

NVDA is headed by the Minister of State in the Government, who is ex-
officio Chairman of NVDA. The Chairman is assisted by the Vice Chairman,

Consumptive use of water means water utilised from available supplies without
return to a water resources system for reuse (e.g. water used in agriculture is not
returned to a stream, river).

by four Chief Engineers (two at Jabalpur and one each at Indore and Sanawad)

3.3 Audit objectives

The department centric audit of NVDA was conducted to assess achievement
of NVDA so far in completion of projects in Narmada basin and plan for
completion of remaining projects by the review year. For this, Audit examined
following aspects;

Identification and planning of projects
Implementation of projects activities as planned
Utilisation of water in completed and ongoing projects
Funding of the projects

Monitoring of project execution

3.5 Scope and methodology of audit

The department centric audit covered review of projects implemented so far,
planning and implementation of the remaining projects and extent of
utilisation of Narmada water. The department centric audit was conducted
during June 2012 to September 2012.

The audit objectives, criteria and methodology were discussed with NVDA
during entry conference in June 2012.

Audit findings were discussed in the exit conference held (December 2012)
with the Vice Chairman, NVDA. Views of the Authority expressed in the exit
conference have been included in this report. The audit findings were sent to
the Government (October 2012). Their reply has not been received (March
2013).

Due to slow progress, there were slippages in completion of major projects.

introduced in May 2007 to indicate all action necessary towards achieving the
targets and efforts required to overcome shortfalls or bottlenecks. All major
projects were planned for completion up to 2011-12 by undertaking

forest clearances and approval of rehabilitation and resettlement plans,
Vision 2015 document (published in 2009) was prepared with a view to
complete all major projects by the year 2015. According to the Vision 2020,
all the major projects have been planned for completion by 2021-22.

3.7.1 Survey and investigation

Survey and investigation activity includes site clearance for survey work,
hydrological survey, dam alignment including grid survey, geological
investigation survey, basin survey, construction material survey including
testing, fixing of levels and submergence survey for resettlement and
rehabilitation (RR) plans,

As of May 2007, out of 23 major projects survey and investigation was not
completed in respect of seven major projects'’. It was seen in audit that survey
and investigation of these seven major projects was still in progress as of
September 2012.

3.7.2 Drawings, designs and DPRs

Drawings, designs and DPRs are prepared on the basis of detailed survey and
investigation for which time period of three months has been provided in the
reverse calendar. As per the Guidelines issued in 2010 by the Central Water
Commission (CWC) for submission, appraisal and clearance of Irrigation and
Multipurpose project, 18 weeks is required for examination of preliminary
reports and six months for project appraisal by Central Water Commission

(CWC).

As per the reverse calendar, approval of DPR component including drawing
and design was to be completed in respect of the above said seven major
Projects during 2007-08. Approval of DPR in respect of these projects could
not be obtained from CWC even till September 2012. As observed in audit,
the delays in obtaining approval from CWC were mainly due to non-
submission of reports in prescribed format, incomplete information,
inadequate drawings etc.

Keeping in view the past experience in approval of DPRs, NVDA will be able
to complete these seven projects before the review year ie. 2024 if it
effectively addresses the shortcomings, such as, non-submission of reports in

months.

issued by MOEF in 2006 requiring prior environmental clearance from the

Keeping in view the actual time taken in obtaining Environment and Forest

In the exit conference NVDA stated (December 2012) that process of
environment clearance included preparation of DPRs, ELA/EMP study, public
hearing and vetting from expert committee. It was further stated that laws for
clearance had been changed and there were delays mainly on part of MOEE as
it takes two to three years to complete the process.

The reply is not acceptable to the extent that submission of incomplete
information and delay in preparation and submission of ELA/ EMP reports by
the Authority to MOEF contributed to delays in clearance from MOEF.
Besides, time taken by MOEF in clearance should also be factored in while
making calendar for completion of projects

3.7.4 Preparation of tender document

Tender documents are prepared after obtaining clearance from MOEF and
technical sanctions from the competent authority. For this activity, a schedule
of two months has been proposed in the reverse calendar 2012 for the
remaining seven projects!*.

Forms of tender documents for item rate contracts are standardised in the
MPWD Manual which was adopted by NVDA. NVDA introduced (August
2005) turnkey contract system with a view to ensure timely completion of
projects. Preparation of tender documents for turnkey contracts was generally
outsourced to consultants. This activity cither in case of item rate contracts or
turnkey contracts had not created bottlenecks in implementation of
programmes for projects.

3.7.5 Land acquisition

As per note 3 below paragraph 2.104 of MPWD Manual, notification for the
acquisition of land required for any particular work must be submitted before
the work is put in hand. In case of turnkey contracts, responsibility of
preparing land acquisition cases has also been entrusted with contractors.

The Ministry of Environment and Forests in the Central Government and the State
Environment Impact Assessment Authority (SEIAA) in the State according to the
category of projects.

8 SMS, Dudhi, Morand-Gunjal, Upper Budner, Chinki, RRB and Ataria.

" SMS, Dudhi, Morand-Gunjal, Upper Budner, Ataria,Chinki and RRB. Out of 23
major projects, seven projects were completed, seven projects were ongoing and two
projects were already in tendering process.

Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2012

NVDA did not analyse delays in land acquisition for projects and did not
consider it as a separate process in execution of a project during preparation of
reverse calendar 2012. Thus, NVDA did not consider land acquisition a major
bottleneck, while making its plan/calendar.

However, we observed that land acquisition had posed hurdles in timely
completion of works in some projects (OSP, ISP, etc.) and in most other
projects only in a small part of the whole land required and did not result in
increasing the total duration of construction during the year 2007-12.

In the exit conference NVDA stated (December 2012) that land acquisition
was a major bottleneck in some areas. Narmada Bachao Andolan (NBA) had
created hindrances by way of court cases which were cleared only after verdict
of Honble High Court and Honble Supreme Court and there were
interference of international organisation and NGOs.

We however noticed that NBA mainly raised issues relating to rehabilitation
and resettlement. Thus, preparation of plans/calendar without considering the
possible impact of rehabilitation and resettlement in land acquisition would
result in time over run in execution of projects affected by litigation and may
result in non-completion of affected projects by the review year.

3.7.6 Fixing of agency

As per clause 2.41 of draft Notice Inviting Tender (NIT) specified in
Appendix 2.10 A of MPWD Manual, validity of offer is six months in case of
tender accepted by the Chief Engineer. Thus, tender should normally be
accepted within a period of six months from the date of submission of tender.

We noticed in test check of 128 agreements entered into between 2007-08 and
2011-12 relating to construction of canals that NVDA took 188 days to 347
days in 10 agreements for acceptance of tenders against the normal period of
six months for its acceptance (Appendix 3.4). We further noticed that in 67
agreements detailed in Appendix 3.5, NVDA took 11 days to 426 days in
issuance of work orders from the date of acceptance of tenders. These delays
contributed to overall delay in the completion of works. Some of the
contractors left the works incomplete. This caused further delays in
completion of works due to extra time involved in retendering and award of
works. This reflected mismanagement in execution of major projects

In the exit conference NVDA stated (December 2012) that there was no delay
in award of work and issue of work order for construction of dams.

The reply is not acceptable as the observations raised in audit relate to the
delays in award of work in construction of canals.

Delays in acceptance of tenders and issuance of work orders contributed to
delays in completion of work. NVDA has to strengthen its mechanism of
evaluation of tenders and award of works to cut short the time being taken for
award of contracts so as to ensure completion of the remaining projects before
the review year. A computerised management information system having
database of all contractors and works executed by them could provide
assistance in finalising award of work to a capable contractor.

3.7.7 Execution of works

After clearance of projects by MOEF and preparation of DPRs, construction of
dams take three to four years as provided in the reverse calendar. As planned
in the reverse calendar, all the major projects in Narmada Basin were to be
completed up to 2011-12. The target could not be achieved due to slippages in
execution of works of dams as well as canals as detailed below;

(2 Dams

WRD constructed five dams'* (capacity: 2.952 MAF) across tributaries of
Narmada river during the years 1956 to 1988 by taking time ranging from
seven to 22 years as shown in Appendix 3.6.

NVDA completed construction of two dams (Man and Jobat) in 2006 and
2007, respectively by taking time of 20 to 23 years. Due to paucity of funds
with NVDA, two dams'® (2.477 MAF) were transferred (May 2000) to
National Hydro Power Development Corporation (NHDC), that completed
these projects within four years.

During 2007-12, one dam viz. Upper Beda (capacity: 101.09 MCM) was
completed (2010) by NVDA in seven years against the scheduled period of
completion of four years as per the reverse calendar. The delay was mainly
due to delay in change of drawing by CWC.

Lower Goi dam (136.65 MCM) taken up for construction in February 2009
and scheduled to be completed in 36 months as per the reverse calendar was
still in progress (March 2013). The delay was mainly due to slow progress by
contractors.

Construction of 13 dams of nine major projects’” for utilisation of 1.819 MAF
water and dams of all the medium and minor projects for utilisation of 4.985
MAF water, were yet to be started.

Considering the delays in past in construction of dams of the projects, it may
not be able to complete all of the remaining 13 dams by the review year unless
NVDA effectively address the problems associated with the construction of
dams, such as slow progress by the contractors and increase in quantities
during execution.

In the exit conference NVDA stated (December 2012) that two dams were
transferred to NHDC due to logistics of finance and human resources and one
of the major dams, namely OSP, was completed within the short span of four
years. NVDA further stated that the agency for Halon project would be fixed
in next meeting of Narmada Control Board (NCB) and in Upper Narmada
Project, affected families had lodged a court case. It was further stated that

reaches as envisaged in reverse calendar. Remaining seven major projects
(1.57 MAF water) can be completed prior to the review year 2024 if all the
clearances are obtained latest by 2018-19, award of works and execution are
undertaken immediately after obtaining clearances and there are no delays in
execution beyond the scheduled completion period of approximately five
years envisaged for these projects.

As all the activities of these seven major projects have to be completed during
the remaining period up to 2024, NVDA has to deploy adequate and suitable
manpower for evaluation of tenders, capability of contractors, feasibility and
close monitoring of programme of execution of works by contractors to ensure
completion within the programmed period. NVDA has to closely monitor the
progress of works by contractors and delay in land acquisition in reaches
affected by litigations and take steps that are necessary for timely completion.

In the exit conference NVDA stated (December 2012) that it was not
practicable and advisable to take up the entire canal network at one go as the
water flow is unidirectional. It was further stated that if contractors at tail end
completed the work and head end remains incomplete then work at tail end
would be ruined by the time work at the head was completed and only main
canal works could be undertaken simultaneously.

The reply is not acceptable as reverse calendar envisaged execution of canal
works including distributaries simultaneously to avoid delay in creation of
irrigation facilities. The issue of contractors at the tail end completing the
work, while the works at the head end remaining incomplete could be
managed through project management techniques such as CPM and PERT.

(iii) Introduction of turnkey contracts

NVDA introduced (August 2005) turnkey contract system with some special
conditions such as interest free mobilisation advance and machinery advance
to enable contractors to immediately start works, deposit of Bank Guarantee
(BG) towards performance security deposit, penalty for not achieving the
specified milestone, incentive for early completion, preparation of cases for
acquisition of land and its follow up, etc. The main objective for introducing
turnkey system of contract was to complete projects by specialised firms
within specified time frame and also to overcome the deficiencies/ hurdles
noticed in item rate contracts.

We noticed that 37 turnkey contracts at total contract value of = 5472.84 crore
were entered into during 2007 to 2012 (Appendix 3.7) for construction of
BDP, ISP, Lower Goi, Punasa LIS, Upper Beda (canal), and Omkareshwar
(OSP) major projects including two contracts for survey and investigation of
medium and minor irrigation projects. Of these, 19 contracts were for
construction works, 12 contracts for consultancy services and six contracts for
survey and investigation works. The scheduled completion period of 18 to 36
months in respect of eight turnkey contracts had already lapsed. All the works
were however, incomplete as of August 2012 after delays of 12 months to 20
months (Appendix 3.8). The main reasons for delay in execution of works as
observed in audit were insufficient resources deployed by the contractors,
award of works to contractors who had defaulted in earlier contracts, slow
progress by the contractors and poor monitoring by NVDA

NVDA, with a view to avoid payment of escalation and to help better
monitoring of projects decided (January 2012) to award works in small
packages and reduced the completion period from 30 months to 18 months.
Till August 2012, no work was awarded in small packages as envisaged.

In the exit conference NVDA stated (December 2012) that it learnt from past
mistakes and floated new tenders on turnkey basis to execute the works of
dams and canals simultaneously. NVDA further stated that imposition of
penalty and payment of incentive had been linked with creation of irrigation
potential.

In the reply, issues of deployment of insufficient resources by the contractors,
award of works to contractors who had defaulted in earlier contracts and slow
progress by the contractors were not addressed.

3.8 Implementation of medium and minor irrigation projects

To utilise 5.39 MAF water out of the award of the total 18.25 MAF water,
construction of 135 medium and 3000 minor projects was planned.

As per Madhya Pradesh Karya Avantan Niyam as amended up to | January
2009, NVDA was entrusted with the work of preparation and execution of all
irtigation projects in Narmada Valley excluding medium and minor projects
and WRD was to execute construction of major, medium and minor irrigation
projects other than in Narmada Basin. NVDA recommended (February 2004)
modifications in Madhya Pradesh Karya Avantan Niyam for entrusting the
works of medium and minor irrigation projects of Narmada Basin to NVDA.

Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2012

As per the Report (May 2009) of the Task Force on Irrigation, Planning
Commission, Government of India, five to 10 years are required for
completion of medium projects. Therefore, all the activities such as, survey
and investigation, preparation of DPRs, various clearances, acquisition of
land, resettlement of project affected families and fixation of agencies should
be completed before 2019 to complete the projects before the review year
2024. After identification of projects, a minimum of 27 months are required
for environment clearance alone which is an external factor on which NVDA
does not have control and two years for other activities.

Considering the minimum actual time of three years in environment clearance
and 12 years in other activities including construction, it may be difficult to
complete the remaining 266 medium projects”! by the review year unless all
the medium projects are identified by 2014, clearances are obtained in
minimum time, funds required for the projects are tied up and other activities
are meticulously planned and executed within the minimum prescribed time.
The mandate for execution of medium and minor projects needs to be made
clear for ensuring completion of the works before 2024.

As per the Report (August 2001) of the Working Group on Minor Irrigation,
Ministry of Water Resources, Government of India, minor projects generally
take three to seven years time in total implementation. Therefore,
implementation of minor projects before the review year is possible if project-
wise plans are prepared, agencies are tied up for execution and funds for the
projects are made available.

The Member (Engineering) stated (October 2012) that 135 medium and 3000
minor projects were to be completed by the WRD against which only 19
medium and 1134 minor projects could be completed. He further explained
that absence of arrangement for timely identification of schemes and detailed
survey of the basin were the reasons for not taking up these projects and that it
had decided to complete survey through basin-wise satellite imageries and
identify the projects for preparation of DPR and detailed estimates

In the exit conference NVDA stated (December 2012) that NBPCL had
undertaken the survey work, which was in progress. NVDA further stated that
arranging finance for execution of these projects was a major area of concern.
The replies do not give details of plan to complete the medium and minor
projects before the review year.

additional water as of August 2012.

Though increase in utilisation of 0.867 MAF water was envisaged, there was

> Construction of BDP project, which is right bank canal of RABLS dam,
envisaged utilisation of 2.035 MAF water. Due to insufficient water in
RABLS dam to cater to the requirement of BDP, a plan to feed water from
reservoir of Ataria major project (0.091 MAF) to BDP was prepared
(January 2009). According to the reverse calendar, the reservoir of Ataria
was planned for completion by the year 2019-20 whereas BDP was planned
to be completed by the year 2016-17. Subsequently, Ataria major project
was converted (January 2009) into minor project due to reduction in
command area at the time of detailed survey to utilise only 0.017 MAF
water. Thus, the requirement of water for BDP would not be met and might
impact the actual drawal of water awarded by NWDT.

Thus, on the one hand there was no plan to make up the decrease in utilisation
(1.334 MAF) of water and use of additional water (0.096 MAF), on the other
hand the proposed increase in utilisation of water (0.867 MAF) may not be
achieved considering the utilisation of water up to 2011-12.

Looking to the slow pace of work of completion of canals system, utilisation
of less water than the created potential and unrealistic change in proposed
utilisation, it may therefore not be possible for the State to utilise the allocated
water by the review year unless factors contributing to delay in completion of
the works are effectively tackled.

The Member (Engineering) stated (October 2012) that the utilisation of water
to full extent could not be achieved as canal work was time consuming and
land acquisition work was spread over more than 2000 villages. In the exit
conference NVDA stated (December 2012) that utilisation of water through
Omkareshwar Project was affected due to hindrances by activists of Narmada
Bacho Andolan (NBA). Regarding increase in utilisation of water in Tawa
project, it was explained that it would be achieved by linking it with Morand-
Gunjal Project. In case of Bargi Diversion Project, it was stated that full
utilisation of water would be achieved after completion of tunnel within four
to five years and construction of up stream reservoirs.

The reply gives details of proposed utilisation of 0.658 MAF water only in
respect of three major projects and not about NVDAs plan to complete
projects so as to utilise the entire allocated water by the review year.

The projects of NVDA are financed through Accelerated Irrigation Benefit
Programme (AIBP), National Bank for Rural and Agricultural Development
(NABARD) and State funds. NVDA incurred total expenditure of 710371.88
crore up to 2006-07. Details of the last five years allotment and expenditure
are given in the table below:

As would be seen from the table, budget provision could not be fully utilised

the last five years were mainly due to delay in approval of detailed project
reports from Central Water Commission (CWC), delay in getting clearance
from Ministry of Environment and Forest (MOEF), delay in fixing of agencies
and slow progress of works by contractors, etc.

According to Vision 2012 (prepared in December 2003), NVDA planned to
complete all major projects by 2012. NVDA assessed total requirement of

the projections. These may cause serious resource constraints during
coming years which may affect timely completion of projects.

> NVDA has never been able to utilise the allotted fund during the last 21
years as shown in Appendix 3.12. During the remaining period of the plan
for completion of remaining projects (that is up to 2021), annual
requirement of funds will range from हैं 1495 crore to हैं 6261 crore. The
details are shown in Appendix 3.10. As the quantum of projects/works and
funds that may have to be handled would increase substantially, NVDA has
to take necessary measures to overcome inadequacies in planning,
scheduling and monitoring of works for projects and consequent delays in
completion of works so as to ensure completion of projects as envisaged in
Vision 2020.

> The NBPCL formed in October 2011 has not come into operation so far

Company. Consequently, process for raising funds for development of
irrigation, power and allied projects etc. could also not be initiated through
NBPCL as of August 2012.

The Member (Engineering) stated (October 2012) that the budget could not be

Accelerated Irrigation Benefit Programme as sufficient large dams for storage
had been constructed and therefore NVDA anticipated problem in funding for
balance projects. It was further stated that the authority even now had resource
constraint to implement all the projects and feasibility of market borrowing
and other funding options through NBPCL was being explored to ensure
timely availability of funds for the projects.

3.11 Monitoring

Monitoring and evaluation are essential components of project execution that
aid completion of projects in a time bound manner with appropriate quality
and economy.

3.11.1 Ineffective monitoring of works by apex level office

The Vision 2012 document envisaged establishment of monitoring cells under
each of the members of NVDA for weekly monitoring and computerisation at
Headquarters as well as in the field formations for quick transmission of
information on the progress of the projects.

We noticed that:

> The Vision 2012 envisaged time event ne twork analysis system in which

various events in a project can be identified within the planned time for
each event and are placed in network showing relationship of cach event to
the other event. The system would enable the department to identify and
reduce the risks in various event of the project and provide a guidance
frame work to develop and implement measures necessary for proper
execution of the projects. This system has not been put in place so far
(August 2012). As a result, mechanism for assessment of risks which arose
during implementation of projects and solutions to address such risks, was
not available at the apex level office to avoid delay in implementation of
projects.

> Monitoring cell was not established as envisaged and there was no system
of regular submission of periodical progress report of works to respective
members at headquarters of NVDA. Progress reports of works were being
submitted by the field formations as and when demanded by the apex
office. As such, execution of works was not being monitored on regular
basis at apex level. In the absence of a system of regular monitoring, apex
level officers will not be able to identify the problems in execution of
works in time and take remedial action. The weakness in monitoring system
may lead to delay in completion of remaining works.

Although NVDA_ has installed computers at field formations and
headquarters, an effective on-line management information system has not
been developed so far (August 2012) to monitor progress of works.
Tn the exit conference NVDA stated (December 2012) that there was a
mechanism for monitoring through reports prescribed from field and monthly/

106


PERT and
CPM were
not being
applied in
execution
of the
projects.

Chapter IIT Department Centric Audit

bimonthly meetings were taken by CE and at NVDA headquarters for
monitoring.

The reply is not acceptable as the reports were not being obtained on regular
basis and in the absence regular returns with up to date information, objectives
of the meetings would not be achieved.

3.11.2 Non-adoption of Programme Evaluation and Review Techniques
(PERT) and Critical Path Method (CPM) for effective monitoring and
evaluation

In the case of turnkey contracts being entered in to for the remaining projects/
remaining works of ongoing projects, a bar chart showing start date,
completion date and duration of completion was being submitted by
contractors as a programme for execution of works. In case of delays,
contractors were being allowed to submit revised construction programme
according to the delays.

As bar chart is a linear representation of construction activities, it does not
indicate the activities that are critical and those which could be undertaken
simultaneously to complete the project in shortest possible time. As a result, it
does not indicate the effect of slippage in any activity from original time
schedule. The monitoring of the projects through Programme Evaluation and
Review Techniques”* (PERT) and Critical Path Method”? (CPM) provide a
solution to this.

We noticed that PERT and CPM was not being applied in NVDA to identify
critical activities of a project so as to ensure close monitoring in respect of
such activities and avoid overall delays.

In the absence of monitoring cells at headquarters of NVDA, regular system of
submission of progress report of works, on-line management information
system and non-application of PERT and CPM for monitoring, evaluation and
alternative plans, management may not be in position to assess the impact of
delays in order to take corrective steps during the course of execution so as to
complete the projects before 2024.

The Member (Engineering) stated (October 2012) that all the projects were
being monitored using management information system (MIS) which is an
advance version of CPM-PERT and was essential to all turnkey contracts. He
further stated that the consultants independently completed MIS and submitted
to NVDA for monitoring of contracts. In the exit conference (December 2012)
NVDA stated that CPM/PERT charts were prepared in NVDA but in case of
slippage of one activity like land acquisition and rehabilitation and
resettlement due to agitation and litigation, the purpose of whole chart was
defeated.

a hic i 5 .

The method which calculates the longest path of planned activities to the end of the

Audit Report Economic (Non-PSUs) Sector for the year ended 31 March 2012

The reply is not convincing as monitoring cell did not exist at headquarters of
NVDA. Regular system of submission of progress report of works to
headquarters of NVDA and use of CPM/PERT were also not noticed in Audit.

3.12 Conclusion

NVDA incurred expenditure of % 5472.40 crore against allotment of
& 6014.88 crore for creation of infrastructure to utilise Narmada water during
2007-12. There were delays in approval of DPRs from CWC, obtaining
environment clearances and award of work. There were further slippages in
execution of dams as well as canals.

Out of the 23 major projects, seven projects have been completed for
utilisation of 3.157 MAF water, seven projects were ongoing and nine projects
were yet to taken up. Further, out of 26 dams of these 23 projects, 11 dams
(storage capacity of 9.114 MAF) for 13 projects were already completed, work
of one dam was in progress and works for 14 dams were yet to be started.

Survey and investigation work for all the remaining medium and minor
projects for utilisation of 3.764 MAF water was still not complete as such
projects are not identified in most cases. Ineffective monitoring, non-
preparation of alternative plans and award of works to ineligible contractors
resulted in delays in construction, As a result, NVDA could increase the
utilisation of water by only 1.63 MAF during 2007-12. Assessment of
requirement of fund for completion of the remaining projects was also
inadequate. Process for raising funds for development of irrigation, power and.
allied projects etc. could not be initiated through Narmada Basin Projects
Company Limited so far.

NVDA may be able to complete construction of remaining dams and canal
works of major projects by the review year only if works are awarded and
taken up immediately and execution of works is monitored meticulously.
Further, remaining medium projects may be completed by the review year if
all the medium projects are identified immediately, clearances are obtained in
minimum time, funds required for the projects are tied up and other activities
are meticulously planned, monitored and executed.

Audit analysis revealed that preparedness for tapping and utilisation of
Narmada water as per NWDT award is tardy as present progress was not
found satisfactory. Effective action would be required to achieve the targets,
else Madhya Pradesh will be lagging behind in getting its due share of
Narmada water.

3.13 Recommendations

The Government, in order to utilise the allocated share of water by the review
year, may consider to;

> prepare a realistic reverse calendar by considering internal as well as
extraneous factors for appropriate planning of works.

108


Chapter III Department Centric Audit

establish a system of identification of risks involved and develop a
mechanism for planning to address such risks to reduce delay in
execution of works by effective monitoring.

examine reasons for delay in environment and forest clearances and
develop a mechanism to reduce delay in obtaining clearances.

strengthen mechanism for evaluation of bids to ensure engagement of
competent contractors.

adopt Programme Evaluation and Review Techniques and Critical Path
Method to ensure synchronised construction of dams and canals.

make realistic assessment of requirements of funds and take steps for
ensuring timely availability of funds.

identify medium projects immediately to take up implementation of
those projects.
